  Infinite Factor Structure in Conditional Asset Pricing
  Mikhail Chernov, Bryan Kelly, Semyon Malamud, and Johannes Schwab∗

                                               This version: October 11, 2026




                                                            Abstract
       The return covariance matrix is widely assumed to have an eigenvalue cliff: a few
       large eigenvalues, then a bounded bulk, as in the approximate factor structure of
       Chamberlain and Rothschild (1983). This premise underlies factor tests, principal-
       component methods, and large-covariance estimation. However, estimating covariances
       from returns pooled across states means viewing a conditional world unconditionally.
       We show that, generically, this view removes the cliff: a conditional model with a few
       factors is, unconditionally, an infinite-factor model in which infinitely many eigenvalues
       diverge. This holds for the characteristic-managed portfolios that machine-learning
       methods trade, and for individual stocks whose loadings differ persistently across firms.
       Yet as long as conditional expected returns admit no asymptotic arbitrage, the maximal
       Sharpe ratio stays finite. For generic expected returns, infinitely many components are
       priced, so no finite set of principal components spans expected returns. Reducing
       returns to a few principal components therefore discards priced risk.

       Keywords: approximate factor structure, managed portfolios, principal components, comovement, machine learning

       JEL: G12, C38, C58

                                                                  1
1    Introduction

The covariance matrix of asset returns is widely assumed to have an eigenvalue cliff: as the
cross-section grows, a few eigenvalues diverge while the rest remain bounded. This spectral
separation defines the approximate factor model of Chamberlain and Rothschild (1983).
Together with the absence of asymptotic arbitrage, it delivers the finite-factor restrictions
on expected returns of the arbitrage pricing theory (APT) of Ross (1976). The same premise
underlies much of empirical practice: tests of factor models, principal-component estimators
of latent factors (Lettau and Pelger, 2020b; Giglio and Xiu, 2021), estimators of the number
of factors, and large-covariance estimation.
    We show that the cliff does not survive the conditioning information on which modern
empirical asset pricing relies. Firm characteristics enter returns in two ways. They set
the weights of large menus of managed portfolios (Kozak, Nagel and Santosh, 2020; Lettau
and Pelger, 2020b; Feng, Giglio and Xiu, 2020; Giglio and Xiu, 2021; Gu, Kelly and Xiu,
2021), and they set the factor loadings of individual stocks. Conditional on the current
characteristics, a few factors may generate all systematic risk. The unconditional second-
moment matrix, however, averages over the movement of characteristics through time, and
that averaging can turn a low-dimensional conditional structure into an unconditional one
with infinitely many diverging eigenvalues. Empirical work cannot sidestep this averaging:
without a model of the conditional structure, a sample covariance matrix pools returns across
states and estimates the unconditional one. The dimension of systematic risk in a given state
is one thing; the dimension generated across states is another.
    Managed portfolios provide the first channel. A single stock can generate infinitely many
linearly independent payoffs by scaling its return with different functions of its characteristics.
Theorem 1 shows that, for a sufficiently rich menu, the eigenvalue at every fixed rank of the
managed-return second-moment matrix grows in proportion to the number of portfolios P .
The result imposes no factor structure on stock returns and holds even for a single stock.

                                                2
Expanding the menu therefore adds new risk directions rather than redundant exposures to
a fixed set of factors. Diversification across stocks need not undo this. If characteristics and
returns were i.i.d. across stocks, managed returns would converge to constants as the number
of stocks N grows. Common variation in characteristics, together with a common return
component, can prevent this collapse: a common state shifts the cross-sectional distribution
of characteristics, and nonlinear portfolio rules turn these shifts into distinct diversified
payoffs. Under these conditions, the divergence survives as N grows.
   Individual stocks provide the second channel. We consider a conditional K-factor model
in which loadings depend on characteristics, so that systematic risk is exactly K-dimensional
at every date (Proposition 2). Observed characteristics need not capture all persistent
differences across firms. Following Gagliardini, Ossola and Scaillet (2016), a latent fixed effect
enters the loadings, so firms with the same observed characteristics can respond differently to
the same common state. When the common state takes infinitely many values, Theorem 3
shows that, generically, the number of unconditional eigenvalues that grow in proportion
to N equals the number of distinct fixed-effect values that persist in the limit. With M
firm types, there are M such eigenvalues; with infinitely many fixed-effect values, there are
infinitely many. A fixed number of conditional factors is thus compatible with infinitely
many strong unconditional directions. The conditional model remains valid; what fails is its
extrapolation to the unconditional second-moment matrix.
   These results change what the absence of arbitrage implies for expected returns. With a
cliff, the absence of asymptotic arbitrage yields an approximate finite-factor representation
of expected returns. Our setting keeps the no-arbitrage premise but not the cliff. Theorems 5
and 6 show that, for generic expected returns, infinitely many groups of principal components
earn risk premia while the maximal Sharpe ratio stays finite; for individual stocks, this
requires persistent firm differences and common states that both take infinitely many values.
A group collects the components whose eigenvalues grow in the same proportion to the size



                                                3
of the cross-section. No finite set of principal components therefore spans expected returns
exactly. Retaining more groups raises the attainable Sharpe ratio toward that of all groups,
but every fixed truncation attains a strictly lower one. The omitted risk also differs from that
in the APT. There, the residual pricing error is idiosyncratic. Here, every omitted priced
group carries systematic risk, because its eigenvalues keep growing with the cross-section.
Truncating the spectrum to a few leading components, as in Lettau and Pelger (2020b);
Giglio and Xiu (2021); Fan, Guo and Zheng (2022), therefore discards priced systematic
risk. Nor are the omitted components weak factors: their eigenvalues are small relative to
the leading ones but still grow in proportion to the number of assets.
   These results offer a unified reading of evidence that appears puzzling under the cliff
assumption. Leading latent factors of individual stocks earn negligible premia, while portfo-
lios hedged against them earn Sharpe ratios above one (Lopez-Lira and Roussanov, 2023).
Premia appear on risk that an approximate factor model classifies as diversifiable (Dello-
Preite, Uppal, Zaffaroni and Zviadadze, 2025), and stochastic discount factors estimated
from large menus load on components far beyond the leading few (Didisheim, Ke, Kelly and
Malamud, 2024). With a cliff, large premia on such small sources of risk imply either near-
arbitrage (Kozak, Nagel and Santosh, 2018) or compensation for idiosyncratic risk. Without
a cliff, they imply neither: the components beyond the leading few carry systematic risk, are
generically priced, and leave the maximal Sharpe ratio finite. In a finite sample, the same
components look like weak factors (Onatski, 2012; Lettau and Pelger, 2020a; Giglio, Xiu and
Zhang, 2025), because their eigenvalues are small relative to the leading ones even though
they grow with the cross-section. Priced residual risk and weak factors are thus two views
of the same structure. Because the framework is general, it identifies which components
are priced but not the size of their premia, which requires more specific assumptions about
expected returns.
   Our results require tracking infinitely many systematic directions at once, as both the



                                               4
portfolio menu and the stock cross-section grow. For managed portfolios, this must work
whatever the return structure of the underlying stocks; for individual stocks, it must turn
a conditional factor model with finitely many factors into an unconditional spectrum with
infinitely many. Standard tools for approximate factor models cannot do this: they count a
fixed number of factors against a bounded remainder. We instead represent each object as
an operator. A menu of managed portfolios acts on the space of payoffs it can generate. The
cross-section of stocks acts on the space of state-response profiles, which record how firms’
factor loadings move with the common state. Eigenvalue growth then becomes a spectral
property of the limiting operator, and risk premia become properties of expected returns
measured against it. To our knowledge, this operator-theoretic approach has not been used
to study factor structure in asset pricing.
   The analysis provides a population mechanism consistent with the empirical virtue of
complexity documented by Kelly, Malamud and Zhou (2024); Kelly, Kuznetsov, Malamud
and Zhang (2026); Didisheim et al. (2024). Expanding a menu of characteristic-managed
portfolios can add both risk and expected return, rather than merely improve the repre-
sentation of a fixed set of factors. This argues for modeling risk premia with many factors
(Didisheim et al., 2024) and for regularizing covariance matrices, for example through the
ridge prior of Kozak et al. (2020), rather than truncating them at a fixed rank. Truncation
discards small-eigenvalue directions, which statistics based on inverse covariance matrices
weight most heavily. Our companion papers develop random-matrix corrections for the bias
that regularization introduces (Chernov, Kelly, Malamud and Schwab, 2025, 2026).
   Our results relate to several strands of the literature. The proliferation of characteristics
and candidate factors is documented by Cochrane (2011); McLean and Pontiff (2016);
Harvey, Liu and Zhu (2016); Feng et al. (2020); Bryzgalova, Huang and Julliard (2023).
The use of conditioning information to construct managed portfolios dates to Hansen and
Richard (1987). Characteristic-based portfolio policies (Brandt, Santa-Clara and Valkanov,



                                               5
2009), semiparametric characteristic factor models (Connor, Hagmann and Linton, 2012),
and conditional-loading models (Kelly, Pruitt and Su, 2019) provide leading examples of the
portfolio rules and return structures considered here. Instrumented principal components
target a conditional stock model, whereas our analysis characterizes the unconditional spectra
of managed portfolios and individual stocks.        Many machine-learning methods likewise
construct characteristic-managed portfolios (Gu et al., 2021; Chen, Pelger and Zhu, 2024;
Bryzgalova, Pelger and Zhu, 2025; Cong, Feng, He and He, 2025).
   A second line of literature studies large cross-sections through principal-component trun-
cation (Giglio and Xiu, 2021; Lettau and Pelger, 2020b). These methods build on the
asymptotic principal-component analysis of approximate factor models developed by Connor
and Korajczyk (1986, 1993). A related literature estimates the number of factors directly
(Bai and Ng, 2002; Onatski, 2009; Ahn and Horenstein, 2013; Fan et al., 2022). Both
approaches rely on a finite-dimensional spectral restriction: beyond a finite number of factors,
the remaining eigenvalues must stay bounded. Our results show that this restriction can fail
for rich menus of managed portfolios and, generically, for individual stock returns when
persistent firm differences and common states both take infinitely many values.
   A third line of literature studies the strength of cross-sectional dependence rather than the
number of factors. Bailey, Kapetanios and Pesaran (2016) summarize dependence through
the growth order of the largest covariance eigenvalue, while Chudik, Pesaran and Tosetti
(2011) formalize intermediate forms of dependence between strong factors and idiosyncratic
noise. Applying this perspective to factor-model residuals, Terenteva, Ahlgren and Antell
(2026) find pervasive comovement after conditioning on standard characteristic factors. Such
evidence shows that a finite factor specification is incomplete. The growth order of the
largest residual eigenvalue does not, however, determine whether the omitted structure
contains finitely or infinitely many directions. Our results characterize the latter possibility:
a rich menu of managed portfolios has infinitely many diverging eigenvalues, and so do



                                               6
unconditional stock returns when persistent firm differences and common states both take
infinitely many values.
        The pricing results are closely related to the question of whether a finite number of
factors spans the stochastic discount factor (Kozak and Nagel, 2024). Our contribution is to
distinguish exact spanning by finitely many principal components from approximation of the
maximal Sharpe ratio in a rich managed-return space. Davis and Lopez-Lira (2026) obtain
a related conclusion from estimation uncertainty in equilibrium, whereas the mechanism
studied here operates in population and does not require estimation uncertainty.
        The remainder of the paper is organized as follows. Section 2 introduces individual
stock returns, managed portfolios, and expanding portfolio menus. Section 3 establishes the
divergence of infinitely many eigenvalues for managed portfolios, both at fixed N and with a
growing stock cross-section. Section 4 imposes a conditional factor model and characterizes
the conditional and unconditional factor structures of individual stocks. Section 5 develops
the pricing and econometric implications, including an exactly solvable Gaussian example,
the pricing of infinitely many groups of principal components, the absence of weak factors,
and covariance matrix estimation. The Appendix contains the proofs.

For managed portfolios, the moment conditions apply to the portfolio returns themselves,
as specified in Assumption 1. Second moments and covariances differ by the rank-one term
µµ′ , with µ = E[rt+1 ], so their eigenvalues interlace (Lemma A.11). We work with second
moments throughout. We write λj (·) for the j-th largest eigenvalue of a symmetric matrix,
counted with multiplicity.
      The APT of Ross (1976) starts from a factor structure for returns. Chamberlain and
Rothschild (1983) characterize an approximate factor structure through the covariance spec-
trum: after a fixed number of leading eigenvalues, the rest stay uniformly bounded as N
grows. When many stocks carry strong exposures to every nonzero combination of the
common factors, those leading eigenvalues diverge. We call the resulting split the eigenvalue
cliff: finitely many diverging eigenvalues above a uniformly bounded remainder. Random
matrix theory studies a close relative, the spiked covariance model, in which a few population
eigenvalues stand apart from a bulk (Johnstone, 2001; Baik, Ben Arous and Péché, 2005).
We borrow its term bulk for the bounded remainder, although our leading eigenvalues diverge
with the dimension rather than staying fixed.
      Empirically, researchers look for the cliff in scree plots and test for it formally (Bai and Ng,
2002; Onatski, 2009, 2010; Ahn and Horenstein, 2013). The formal procedures ask whether
a fixed, finite number of eigenvalues separates from the bulk, and Connor and Korajczyk
  2                                                                                               ′
   Second moments also arise in the tradable stochastic discount factor normalized as mt+1 = 1 − rt+1 a.
Remark A.12 shows that this discount factor is proportional to the one built from covariances.




                                                   8
(1993) and Bai and Ng (2002) apply them to individual-stock returns. Others take the cliff
as given: Fan, Liao and Mincheva (2013) impose an approximate factor structure to estimate
the covariance matrix of individual stocks. The cliff is also imposed on portfolio returns.
Lettau and Pelger (2020b) assume a low-dimensional factor structure for 370 decile portfolios
sorted on anomaly characteristics. Giglio and Xiu (2021) require the factor eigenvalues of 647
portfolios to grow linearly with their number. Fan et al. (2022) impose the same structure to
estimate the number of factors in 100 portfolios double-sorted on size and book-to-market.


2.2        Managed portfolios

A managed portfolio assigns to stock i a weight that depends on that stock’s own characteris-
tics, for example tilting toward stocks with high book-to-market ratios, small capitalization,
or high momentum. A menu of P such portfolios, f (P ) , is a collection of weighting functions

series that can be studied with standard asset-pricing tools. Those tools estimate uncondi-
tional moments, because a time series of returns pools observations across states. Our object

         ΨP = E[Rt+1 Rt+1 ] ∈ RP ×P .                                                                     (3)


       The spectrum of ΨP depends on whether the weights respond to states. Static portfolios,
whose weights stay fixed across states, cannot add payoffs: N independent static portfolios
generate the same payoffs as the N underlying stocks. With more than N of them, some
returns are exact combinations of others, and ΨP has zero eigenvalues. In the APT of Ross
(1976) and Chamberlain and Rothschild (1983), the cross-section expands by adding assets.
Adding static portfolios is no substitute.
       Managed portfolios are different. Even a single traded asset can support arbitrarily
many independent managed returns when its characteristics take infinitely many values,
because the weights can respond differently to each characteristic value.5 The positive
definite conditional second moment assumed above ensures that distinct weighting functions
produce distinct returns. Consequently, a growing menu can add new payoffs even when
the underlying stock universe is fixed.6 Assumption 1 controls how the menu behaves as it
expands and requires it to retain exposure to these payoffs.

weighting functions with random labels drawn independently of the panel, the conditions hold
almost surely conditional on the label sequence. Every Rk,t+1 is square integrable.7 There is
a constant Cmenu < ∞, the same for every P and every arbitrary managed portfolio, such

                                                  11
supply the required payoff variety (Lemma D.4). The assumption states payoff variety di-
rectly, so it also covers stocks with infinite unconditional second moments, provided there are
arbitrarily many independent square-integrable managed returns. Appendix C.2 introduces
the menus used in empirical asset pricing: random features (Didisheim et al., 2024), fixed
bases, and sorts and trees (Fama and French, 1993; Daniel, Grinblatt, Titman and Wermers,
1997; Moritz and Zimmermann, 2016; Bryzgalova et al., 2025; Cong et al., 2025). For sorts
and trees we use expected-count versions, whose weights use the expected rather than the
realized number of stocks in a cell. Appendix F verifies Assumption 1 for these constructions
at a fixed N . Appendix G supplies uniform-in-N menu bounds; the conditions for a growing
cross-section are collected in Assumption 2.


3    The portfolio eigenvalue cliff fails

The eigenvalue at every fixed rank of the managed-return second-moment matrix grows
proportionally to the number of portfolios P (Theorem 1 below). We write ΨP,N for ΨP
when displaying the number of underlying stocks. For fixed N , Assumption 1 suffices: there
are arbitrarily large collections of linearly independent, square-integrable managed returns,
and the expanding menu retains exposure to them. No factor structure is required for the
underlying stock returns.
    An additional condition is needed when the stock cross-section grows. To see why, suppose
the return-characteristic pairs are independent and identically distributed across stocks. For
each fixed weighting fk , the law of large numbers gives

      ΨP,N −→ āP ā′P            as N → ∞.


The limiting returns are deterministic. Their second-moment matrix has rank at most one,
reflecting mean returns rather than stochastic comovement. Cross-sectional diversification
can thus remove all comovement among managed returns, leaving at most one positive
eigenvalue.
   Common variation in characteristics, combined with a common return component, can
prevent this collapse. Assumption 2 states the required conditions informally; Appendix D
gives the formal version.

     Condition (i) requires arbitrarily many linearly independent payoff directions to survive
diversification. Condition (ii) requires the common state to summarize the information
in characteristics relevant for conditional portfolio second moments in the large-N limit.
Condition (iii) prevents the menu’s average squared exposure to a nonzero managed return
from vanishing relative to that return’s second moment. Section 4 constructs a conditional
factor model for stocks around this common state.

When Nmax = ∞, we denote these growth constants by κj . Both (10) and (11) concern
fixed j and take the limit in P before the limit in N when the stock cross-section grows
(Remark H.11).
      Theorem 1 rules out an unconditional approximate factor structure in the spectral sense
of Chamberlain and Rothschild (1983). Such a structure would require all but finitely many
covariance eigenvalues to remain bounded as the menu expands.8 In contrast, Theorem 1
implies that, however many leading principal components are removed, the largest remaining
eigenvalue still grows proportionally to P .
      Theorem 1 reflects the behavior of the whole matrix: ΨP grows proportionally to P , and
ΨP /P settles, in the sense of (5), to a limit with infinitely many positive eigenvalues. The
eigenvalue at every fixed rank therefore grows proportionally to P .

    Condition (7) makes these limiting eigenvalues positive. The limiting weighting sends
no nonzero managed return to zero, and there are arbitrarily many linearly independent
managed returns, so it has infinitely many positive eigenvalues. The eigenvalue of ΨP /P at
every fixed rank therefore stays bounded away from zero, while (6) bounds it from above.


4    Individual stocks in a conditional factor model

Individual stocks can lose the unconditional cliff as well, but only when firms differ persis-
tently. A symmetric benchmark shows why. Suppose that


         ri,t+1 = B(Xi,t )Gt+1 .


Given a common state Wt , let characteristics be independent draws from the same dis-
tribution, and let the factor be independent of those draws. Stocks then have identical
second moments, and every pair of distinct stocks has the same co-moment: E[ri,t+1 rj,t+1 ]
is independent of (i, j). This symmetry gives every zero-cost portfolio a bounded second
moment when its weights have unit length. Hence E[rr′ ] has at most one eigenvalue growing
like N , with the equal-weighted market portfolio as eigenvector. Whether that one eigenvalue



                                                   16
actually grows depends on the market portfolio. As N grows, the equal-weighted market
return converges to E[B(Xi,t ) | Wt ] Gt+1 , the average loading times the factor. Provided
the factor’s second moment is nondegenerate, the market eigenvalue therefore grows like N
exactly when the average loading is nonzero with positive probability (Remark B.10).
      Persistent differences in loadings can break this symmetry. We represent them by stock
fixed effects: firm attributes not captured by observed characteristics. Stock-specific co-
efficients of this kind are common in conditional asset pricing (Gagliardini et al., 2016).
When the common state takes infinitely many values, the number of eigenvalues of the
unconditional second-moment matrix ΣN that grow like N generically equals the number of
distinct fixed-effect values that persist in the limit.

Assumption 3 (Conditional factor model with fixed effects) Each stock has a fixed
effect Si ∈ T . The fixed effects form a nonrandom infinite array (Si )i≥1 . As more stocks
are included, their mix of fixed effects approaches a distribution ν. Let M be the size of
its support. The array itself may contain more distinct values than remain in this limiting
support.
      Each period has a common state Wt , as in Assumption 2, with the same distribution PW
at every date. Given that state, characteristics are independent across stocks and follow the
same distribution for every fixed effect.9
      Stock returns follow the K-factor model


        ri,t+1 = B(Xi,t , Si )′ Gt+1 + εi,t+1 ,   i = 1, . . . , N.                               (14)


Conditional on the date-t characteristics and fixed effects (Xt ; S) ≡ (Xi,t , Si )i≤N , the factors
  9
    This distribution can change with the common state. Assumption B.3 in Appendix B states the precise
regularity conditions.




                                                    17
have mean and second moment


         E[Gt+1 | Xt , S] = µG (Xt ; S),             E[Gt+1 G′t+1 | Xt , S] = Ω(Xt ; S).             (15)


The mean vector µG and second-moment matrix Ω depend on the cross-section only through
the empirical distribution of characteristics and fixed effects. Relabeling the stocks leaves
these moments unchanged.10
       The residuals are conditionally mean zero, independent of the factors, and uncorrelated
across stocks:


         E[εi,t+1 εj,t+1 | Xt , S] = δi,j σε2 (Xi,t , Si ),                                          (16)


where δi,j equals one if i = j and zero otherwise, and, for constants 0 < σ 2 ≤ σ̄ 2 < ∞,


         σ 2 ≤ σε2 (x, s) ≤ σ̄ 2


for almost every x and every s.

       Persistent firm differences affect loadings through B(Xi,t , Si ), so firms with the same
observed characteristics may still have different loadings. Given the common state, charac-
teristics follow the same distribution across fixed effects. The fixed effect plays the role for
stocks that the portfolio index plays for a menu: B(·, Si ) selects the loading function of stock
          (P )
i, as fk         selects the weighting function of portfolio k. The number of distinct fixed effects
therefore plays the role of the number of distinct weightings.
       With finitely many fixed-effect values, the stocks form firm types: all stocks of one type
share one value. With a single value (M = 1), persistent heterogeneity disappears, provided
every stock carries that value; otherwise a vanishing share of stocks could still carry other
  10
       Their dependence on N is suppressed; Appendix B imposes the limits needed as the stock count grows.



                                                              18
values. With infinitely many values, exposures can differ persistently across arbitrarily many
fixed-effect values.
   Let

                                                        ′
      BN (Xt ; S) = B(X1,t , S1 ), . . . , B(XN,t , SN ) ∈ RN ×K


                                                                     ′
denote the matrix of factor loadings, and let ΣN (Xt ; S) := E[rt+1 rt+1 | Xt , S]. The condi-
tional mean-zero and factor-independence restrictions on residuals in Assumption 3 imply




      ΣN (Xt ; S) = BN (Xt ; S)Ω(Xt ; S)BN (Xt ; S)′ + diag σε2 (Xi,t , Si ) .
                                                                            
                                                                                          (17)


   The conditional second-moment matrix of individual stocks has a strong-factor structure.

Proposition 2 (Strong K-factor structure of stock returns) Suppose Assumption 3
and Assumption B.3(i)–(iv) hold. Then the ordered eigenvalues of


                            ′
      ΣN (Xt ; S) = E[rt+1 rt+1 | Xt , S]


satisfy:

                                                            
  (i) For k = 1, . . . , K, the limit limN →∞ λk ΣN (Xt ; S) /N exists and is strictly positive
      almost surely.

 (ii) For k = K + 1, . . . , N ,


            σ 2 ≤ λk ΣN (Xt ; S) ≤ σ̄ 2
                                



      uniformly in N .



                                                  19
       Proposition 2 is the conditional counterpart of the approximate factor structure of Cham-
berlain and Rothschild (1983). Their definition requires only that the first K eigenvalues be
unbounded. Proposition 2 delivers the stronger version assumed by Bai and Ng (2002), in
which these eigenvalues grow linearly with N and the rest stay bounded. Conditional on the
current cross-section, K factors therefore span the systematic component of stock returns.
The conditioning matters: the unconditional second moment averages over realizations of the
cross-section, and this averaging need not preserve the count K. Because a sample covariance
matrix pools returns across realizations of the cross-section, it estimates this unconditional
moment rather than the conditional one.
       Unconditional averaging can leave stocks with a single growing eigenvalue, with finitely
many, or with infinitely many. The responses b(s, w) = E[B(Xi,t , s) | Wt = w] are the
average factor loadings of a firm with fixed effect s when the common state is w; they are
the same for every stock i because characteristics share one distribution given the state.
Genericity here concerns these response functions, with the laws of the common state and
the fixed effects held fixed; Definition B.5 gives its precise meaning. Infinitely many growing
eigenvalues require two sources of variation. First, persistent firm differences must retain
an infinite range as the number of stocks grows: infinitely many distinct fixed effects are
not enough if all but finitely many of them lose their share of the cross-section. Second,
the common state must take infinitely many values. For generic responses of firms to the
common state, these two conditions also suffice.11 The next theorem makes this statement
precise for the stocks’ unconditional second-moment matrix.

Theorem 3 (The unconditional stock cliff fails) Let Assumptions 3 and B.3 hold. If
both the distribution of the common state, PW , and the limiting distribution of fixed effects,
  11
     The exceptions are knife-edge cases. For example, infinitely many eigenvalues fail to grow if the responses
of all firm types are exact combinations of the responses of finitely many of them. An arbitrarily small change
in the responses breaks such an exact relation.




                                                      20
ν, have infinite support, then, for generic response configurations,

                      ′
                            
           λj E[rt+1 rt+1 ]
       lim                    >0                                                             (18)
      N →∞       N

for every fixed j.
    If ν has M < ∞ support points, at most M eigenvalues satisfy (18). If also PW has
infinite support, exactly M do for generic configurations. If the fixed-effect array takes exactly
M distinct values, each with positive limiting share, the N −M eigenvalues on the within-type
contrast directions stay bounded and converge to at most M finite levels.

    Because the Si are fixed, the unconditional second moment averages over time while
holding firm identities constant. Stocks with the same fixed effect are interchangeable. With
finitely many types, the eigenvectors of the growing eigenvalues are combinations of the
equal-weighted portfolios within each type. A type average need not itself be a principal
component.
    Averaging is also what makes the conditional and unconditional factor counts differ.
Within a fixed effect, characteristics still generate cross-sectional variation in loadings,
supporting the K conditional factors of Proposition 2. Averaging over characteristics removes
the stock-specific identities of these exposures and leaves a bounded component within each
fixed effect. The responses b(s, w) determine the growing eigenvalues, so the number of
strong unconditional stock factors depends on firm heterogeneity and state variation, not on
the conditional factor count K.


5    Implications

Sections 3 and 4 show that managed portfolios have no eigenvalue cliff under the stated
conditions and that stocks generically lose it when persistent firm differences and common
states both take infinitely many values. Four implications follow. First, an exactly solvable


                                               21
Gaussian model delivers the entire spectrum in closed form. For both random features and
partitions, the spectrum descends as a staircase. For random features, the staircase becomes
shallower as the weights become more nonlinear. Partitions produce a flatter staircase
than random features for every degree of nonlinearity. The remaining three implications
do not rely on the Gaussian model. Second, infinitely many groups of principal components
generically earn nonzero expected returns while the maximal Sharpe ratio stays finite. Third,
the spectrum contains no weak factors: what looks like a weak factor is a strong factor with
a small growth constant. Fourth, covariance estimation cannot assume a fixed number of
factors with a bounded remainder: every fixed truncation leaves out diverging eigenvalues.


5.1     An exactly solvable case: the shape of the spectrum

Theorem 1 shows that managed portfolios have no eigenvalue cliff, but it does not say what
spectrum replaces the cliff. With Gaussian characteristics and a Gaussian-shaped conditional
second moment of the factor, the growth constants of all eigenvalues have a closed form: they
form a geometric staircase. The staircase looks like a cliff when the menu is close to linear,
and it becomes shallower as the menu becomes more nonlinear, as characteristics become
more correlated across stocks, or as factor risk becomes less concentrated.
      Consider the conditional factor model of Assumption 3 in its simplest form. Each stock
has d characteristics driven by a common Gaussian state:

               √     p                    i.i.d.
        Xi =    ρ W + 1 − ρ ηi ,     W, ηi ∼ N (0, Id ).                                 (19)


The parameter ρ ∈ (0, 1) is the correlation between the same characteristic of two different
stocks. It is also the share of each characteristic’s variance that comes from the common
state W . Returns follow the one-factor model


        ri = G + εi ,                                                                    (20)


                                                   22
where the residuals satisfy Assumption 3, so their conditional variances are bounded above
and away from zero. Conditional on W , the factor G is independent of the characteristic
draws.
   Following Didisheim et al. (2024), who adapt the random Fourier features of Rahimi and
Recht (2007), we first construct managed portfolios from random Fourier features. Each
portfolio carries a label θ = (ω, b). Labels are drawn independently across portfolios and
independently of the return-characteristic panel, with


     ω ∼ N (0, γ 2 Id ),         b ∼ U[0, 2π],    γ > 0.


The label fixes the weight function:

                                                                       N
                  √          ′              (P )                   1 X (P )
     f (x; θ) =       2 cos(ω x + b),      fk = f (·; θk ),   Rk =      f (Xi ) ri .      (21)
                                                                   N i=1 k

The bandwidth γ controls how sharply the portfolio weights respond to characteristics and,
therefore, how nonlinear they are. These portfolios are a special case of the random-feature
menu of Appendix C.2.1.
   In parallel with random features, we also study partition menus. Portfolio sorts have
a nonparametric partitioning interpretation (Cattaneo, Crump, Farrell and Schaumburg,
2020; Cattaneo, Crump, Feng and Wang, 2026). Divide the characteristic space into P cells
of equal probability under the cross-sectional distribution N (0, Id ): multivariate analogs of
double- and triple-sorts used in asset pricing. The portfolio for a cell assigns weight P/N
to each stock in that cell and zero to every other stock. The weight uses the expected cell
count N/P rather than the realized count, so it stays P/N however many stocks the cell
holds, and an empty cell gives a zero portfolio. Refine the cells by successive bisection, so
P = 2n : cycle through the d characteristics, splitting every cell at the median, within that
cell, of the selected characteristic. Unlike the random-feature menu, the partition menu


                                                   23
has no nonlinearity parameter. The menu grows richer only as the cells are refined. See
Appendix C.2.3 for technical details on such partition menus.
   A portfolio’s return splits into a systematic part, shared across stocks through the factor
G, and idiosyncratic noise that diversification removes. In a large cross-section (as N → ∞),
only the systematic part survives, and each portfolio return becomes a position in the factor
G whose size depends on the common state:

                                                (P )
       lim Rk = f¯k (W ) G,        f¯k (W ) = E fk (Xi ) | W ,
                                                            
                                                                                              (22)
      N →∞



almost surely. For random features, f¯k (W ) = f¯(θk ; W ) with f¯(θ; W ) = E[f (Xi ; θ) | W ], the
notation of Appendix K. The limiting spectrum is determined by the second moments of
these loadings across states, weighted by the factor’s conditional second moment.
   The second-moment matrix ΨP = E[RR′ ] depends on the factor only through its condi-
tional second moment, which we take to be

                              2
      E[G2 | W ] = e−a∥W −s∥ ,       a ≥ 0,      s ∈ Rd .                                     (23)


Here a sets how tightly factor risk concentrates in the state space, and s locates it. When
a is close to zero, E[G2 | W ] is close to a constant. The second moment peaks at one when
W = s, and when a is large it decays quickly as W moves away from s.
   The conditional factor premium E[G | W ] may also vary with W , subject only to the
automatic bound E[G | W ]2 ≤ E[G2 | W ]; it does not affect the second-moment spectrum.
   To index the steps of the spectrum, define

                
             m+d

Step m comprises the ranks Jm−1 < j ≤ Jm . Write cfeat and csort for the leading growth



                                                24
constants of the random-feature menu and partition menu, respectively. Write Qfeat,a and
Qsort,a for the ratios of their second growth constants to their first. The scales give the height
of the first step; the theorem establishes that the same ratios govern every subsequent step.

Theorem 4 (The spectrum for random features and partitions) Assume (19) with
ρ ∈ (0, 1), returns ri = G + εi satisfying Assumption 3, and (23). Conditional on W , let G
be independent of the characteristics. For either the random-feature menu (21) with γ > 0,
or the equal-probability partition menu with P = 2n that bisects the characteristic coordinates
in turn, the limits κj in (11) with Nmax = ∞ exist, almost surely over the labels for random
features, and, for m = 0, 1, 2, . . . and Jm−1 < j ≤ Jm , satisfy


         (a) random features:            κj = cfeat Qm
                                                     feat,a ,

                  (b) partitions:        κj = csort Qm
                                                     sort,a ,



where cfeat , csort > 0 do not vary with j and Qfeat,a , Qsort,a ∈ (0, 1).

       Consistent with Theorem 1,12 the eigenvalue of ΨP at every fixed rank grows like P :
λj (ΨP ) ∼ P κj . Theorem 4 gives the growth constants κj exactly. They form a geometric
staircase (Figure 1). Step m holds m+d−1
                                         
                                    d−1
                                           eigenvalues with a common growth constant: one
for m = 0, d for m = 1, and d(d + 1)/2 for m = 2. The steps grow in size when d > 1; for
d = 1, each contains one eigenvalue. When the number of characteristics d is large, step m
holds about dm /m! eigenvalues.
       Each eigenvector v = (vk )Pk=1 of the matrix ΨP corresponds to a managed portfolio

partitions with constant factor risk (a = 0), they are the Hermite polynomials themselves

   The eigenfunctions line up with the steps of the staircase. For partitions with a = 0 the
match is exact; for random features, it holds for the Hermite polynomial that multiplies the
bell-shaped Gaussian weight. Step m = 0 holds the largest eigenvalue, whose eigenfunction
is a constant: the corresponding portfolio return is the factor G itself. Step m = 1 holds
d equal eigenvalues, which correspond to factor-timing strategies Wℓ G, ℓ = 1, . . . , d, linear
in the common state. Step m = 2 holds d(d + 1)/2 equal eigenvalues, which correspond
to strategies Wℓ1 Wℓ2 G quadratic in the state, and so on. Each step thus captures a higher
degree of nonlinearity in the managed portfolios, and each falls below the one above it by
the same factor, Qfeat,a for random features and Qsort,a for partitions. The contribution of
each degree of nonlinearity to managed-portfolio risk therefore decays exponentially in m.
   Equation (K.26) in Appendix K gives Qfeat,a and Qsort,a in closed form. Qfeat,a increases
with γ by (K.27): the more nonlinear the random features, the more higher-order powers
of W contribute to managed-portfolio risk. Both Qfeat,a and Qsort,a are monotone increasing
in ρ (the importance of the common shock) and vanish when ρ = 0: without a common
characteristic shock, the diversified spectrum has rank one, leaving only the eigenvalue of
the factor G itself. Both decay ratios fall as factor risk becomes more concentrated, that is,
as a rises. The location s affects only the scales cfeat and csort .
   We also show that


      Qfeat,a (γ, ρ) < Qsort,a (ρ) ,                                                       (24)


no matter how big γ is (Proposition K.9(i′ )). In this sense, partition menus are more
nonlinear than any random-feature menu: higher powers of W matter more for their portfolio
risk, so multivariate sorts produce a shallower staircase.

a one-factor model, ΨP has d + 1 dominant eigenvalues that may appear to be the only ones
growing like P . Yet all other eigenvalues grow like P as well, only with a much smaller
growth constant κj . In the population, increasing γ raises the relative sizes κj /κ1 of the
higher steps and replaces the apparent cliff with a shallow staircase. It also shrinks the
common systematic scale cfeat .


5.2       Risk premia: infinitely many components are priced

The eigenvalue results concern risk. Which components earn premia is a separate question.
Infinitely many components can be priced even when the maximal Sharpe ratio is finite. For
generic expected returns and a fixed stock count, every finite selection of leading managed-
portfolio components then leaves a Sharpe-ratio shortfall, although a long enough selection
makes it arbitrarily small. Measuring that shortfall requires a full benchmark.
      Write µP,N = E[Rt+1 ] for the mean vector of the P menu returns with N stocks. An
investor choosing weights a ∈ RP receives a′ Rt+1 , with mean a′ µP,N and variance a′ ΨP,N a −
(a′ µP,N )2 . Let Sfull be the supremum of the Sharpe ratios available from all such choices of
weights and menu sizes:

                                              (a′ µP,N )2

                          a′ ΨP,N a>0

grows, the supremum includes every stock count in the specified sequence.13

The growth constants in (11), denoted by κj , then exist and are positive by Theorem 1.
       We compare this full benchmark with portfolios that use only leading principal-

Theorem 5 (Infinitely many managed-portfolio components are priced) Impose

      SJ < Ssys    for every finite J,    SJ ↑ Ssys ≤ Sfull    as J → ∞.


If Nmax < ∞, then Ssys = Sfull . For Nmax = ∞, any µ ∈ P(Σ) with Sfull < ∞ satisfying
Definition J.2 has Ssys = Sfull .

   For generic means and a fixed stock count, no finite number of leading groups attains
Sfull , but enough groups can get as close to it as desired.
   The equality Ssys = Sfull has a concrete portfolio meaning. Under the theorem’s as-
sumptions, any portfolio available from the full menu can be approximated by combining
sufficiently many limiting groups: both its expected return and its variance can be matched



                                               32
arbitrarily closely. That approximation is why allowing J to grow recovers the full Sharpe
benchmark.16
       For individual stocks, we ask the same questions about static portfolios. Hold fixed the
unconditional second moments from Theorem 3, and write


                      ′

The superscript r marks quantities built from stock returns. The full benchmark Srfull allows
any static stock portfolio, with any stock count in the sequence:

for which the expected return on every portfolio that weights stocks by a bounded continuous
function of their fixed effects converges as more stocks are included.17 Set P r (Σ) = P0r (Σ).
As for managed portfolios, δ > 0 bounds the squared Sharpe ratio of every static stock
portfolio by (1 − δ)/δ.
       Let κrj = limN →∞ λj (ΣN )/N be the stock growth constants. For a positive value κrE , fix a

As for managed portfolios, SrJ is the best limiting Sharpe ratio using only the first J positive
groups, ordered by decreasing growth constant, and Srsys is its supremum as more groups are
included. Let xrJ sum their squared uncentered Sharpe ratios and xrsys sum over all positive
groups. If fewer than J groups exist, retain all of them; an empty sum is zero. Thus

                X                                xrJ                            xrsys
      xrsys =        SR2,r
                       E,∞ ,       (SrJ )2 =           ,        (Srsys )2 =             ,   (32)

with the same convention at an uncentered ratio of one. The stock recovery conditions
impose the same two requirements as for managed portfolios: a vanishing contribution from
omitted components and a full optimum that does not fall as stocks are added. They are
stated in Definition J.4 in Appendix J.2. Genericity in P r (Σ) and Pδr (Σ) has the same
meaning as for managed portfolios, with the metric in Definition J.3.

Theorem 6 (Unconditional stock components are priced) Let (ΣN )N be the uncon-
ditional second-moment family of a stock model satisfying the assumptions of Theorem 3.
For every such family and fixed-effect array, a generic µ̄ ∈ P r (Σ) satisfies


      SR2,r
        E,∞ > 0         for every group E with κrE > 0,               (Srfull )2 < ∞.


If PW and ν have infinite support, there are infinitely many such groups for generic response
configurations. For every δ ∈ (0, 1), the same pricing conclusion is generic in Pδr (Σ), and




                                                           34
(Srfull )2 ≤ (1 − δ)/δ throughout that class. On the same generic sets,


         SrJ ↑ Srsys ≤ Srfull   as J → ∞.


If there are infinitely many positive groups, then SrJ < Srsys for every finite J. Any µ̄ ∈ P r (Σ)
with Srfull < ∞ satisfying Definition J.4 has Srsys = Srfull .

       For generic stock means, the number of positive groups matters. If there are only finitely
many, using all of them attains Srsys . If there are infinitely many, every finite selection omits
a priced group whose premium can improve the Sharpe ratio. Even then, including enough
groups makes the remaining shortfall relative to Srsys as small as desired. The number of
groups is determined by second moments; their pricing depends on expected returns.
       When the stock count grows, approaching Ssys or Srsys need not recover the full optimum.
As more stocks enter, a sequence of portfolios can retain a substantial Sharpe ratio yet involve
components farther and farther down the spectrum. Every fixed list of leading groups then
misses this contribution in the limit, although an investor allowed to use all components can
still earn it. The recovery conditions rule out a persistent contribution of this kind. They
also require the full optimum not to fall with the stock count, since Sfull and Srfull include
opportunities at smaller stock counts as well.18 Under these additional conditions, keeping
more groups approaches the full Sharpe benchmark in both theorems.
       Classical APT combines a low-dimensional covariance structure with the absence of
asymptotic arbitrage to obtain a finite-dimensional model of expected returns up to bounded
total squared pricing errors (Ross, 1976). Managed portfolios keep the absence of asymptotic
arbitrage but lose the low-dimensional covariance structure, and by Theorem 5 their expected
returns generically load on infinitely many principal-component groups. Individual stocks
  18
    For stocks, a higher optimum at a finite stock count can occur even when every mean vector lies in the
leading eigenspace; see (J.29) in Appendix J.2. For a consistently specified stock universe, adding stocks
cannot lower the static optimum because the new stocks can receive zero weight. For managed portfolios,
the analogous nesting must hold for the spaces of attainable managed returns.



                                                   35
lose that structure too when persistent firm differences and the common state both take
infinitely many values, and Theorem 6 then gives the same conclusion. No finite collection
of those groups spans expected returns exactly. With a finite maximal Sharpe ratio, a single
payoff in the L2 closure of the return space can still price every return in that space. For
finite cross-sections, Filipović and Schneider (2026) characterize conditional representations
with traded factors, regression loadings, and unpriced residual risk. Under the law of one
price, such representations attain the maximal conditional Sharpe ratio.
   This failure of finite spanning is consistent with the out-of-sample evidence of Didisheim
et al. (2024): their stochastic discount factor continues to load on factors far beyond the
leading few, and truncating it to five principal components cuts its out-of-sample Sharpe
ratio from 3.7 to about 1.3. The near-arbitrage argument of Kozak et al. (2018) does not
conflict with Theorem 5. It bounds the maximal Sharpe ratio, which the theorem keeps
finite, and it delivers a few-factor stochastic discount factor only when a few components
dominate the covariance matrix. Without a cliff, the same bound leaves room for infinitely
many priced groups.
   The pricing of infinitely many groups is also consistent with Lopez-Lira and Roussanov
(2023). The first fifty latent factors of individual stock returns capture virtually all of
their common time-series variation, yet exposure to these factors earns negligible premia,
while a characteristic-based portfolio hedged against them earns a Sharpe ratio above one.
Theorems 5 and 6 say that infinitely many groups are priced, not how large each premium
is, so they are consistent with this pattern but do not predict its magnitude.
   Dello-Preite et al. (2025) also find premia beyond the leading factors, from the opposite
covariance premise. They retain an approximate factor structure and let expected returns
compensate investors for diversifiable risk. In our setting each fixed principal component
retains risk as the menu expands: its second moment grows like P with a positive growth
constant. Our results suggest that premia attributed to unsystematic risk in an approximate



                                              36
factor model may reflect components deep in a spectrum without a cliff. We do not show
that the two sets of premia coincide.
      Davis and Lopez-Lira (2026) also find that many components are priced, but the source
is estimation uncertainty in equilibrium. As the number of assets grows, pricing errors scale
with N/T , and the estimated stochastic discount factor loads mainly on estimation error
rather than on fundamental risk. Ours is a population result that survives at T = ∞, driven
by cross-sectional comovement in characteristics. The two mechanisms are complementary:
even with infinite data, infinitely many groups of principal components are generically priced,
and finite samples add estimation error on top.


5.3     No weak factors

In an approximate factor model, a factor is strong if the corresponding eigenvalue of the
return covariance matrix grows proportionally with the number of assets. A weak factor’s
eigenvalue remains bounded or grows more slowly (Onatski, 2012; Lettau and Pelger, 2020a;
Bailey et al., 2016; Giglio et al., 2025).
      The classification presumes the cliff: finitely many factors set against a remainder of
bounded eigenvalues. The cliff survives in two settings. Conditional on date-t characteristics,
stock returns have K eigenvalues that grow like N , and the other N − K stay bounded
(Proposition 2). Unconditionally, N − M eigenvalues stay bounded when firms come in
M types (Theorem 3). In both settings, the classification applies. It does not apply to
managed portfolios: by Theorem 1, the eigenvalue of ΨP,N at every fixed rank grows like
P , and its growth constant stays positive as the number of stocks grows. Nor does it apply
to unconditional stock returns when fixed effects and common states both take infinitely
many values: the eigenvalue at every fixed rank then grows like N for generic response
configurations (Theorem 3). Neither object has a weak factor.
      In a finite sample, empirical analysis labels a component weak if its eigenvalue is deemed



                                                37
small relative to the leading ones. A small eigenvalue need not be bounded: it can grow like
P with a small growth constant. For example, in the Gaussian model of Theorem 4, each
step of the staircase lies below the previous one by the decay ratio. In Figure 1, with d = 50
characteristics and the narrow bandwidth, the decay ratio is about 0.01. The first two steps
hold d + 1 = 51 components, close to the market portfolio and the d portfolios linear in
the characteristics. Every later component is at least two orders of magnitude smaller than
these 51, so the spectrum drops sharply after rank 51. A finite-factor reading treats the
components after the drop as weak or as noise, yet every one of them grows like P .
   These components are also priced: for generic expected returns, every group with a
positive growth constant has a positive Sharpe ratio, and infinitely many groups qualify
(Theorem 5; Theorem 6 gives the parallel result for stocks). A component that empirical
analysis labels weak is therefore a strong component with a small growth constant: its eigen-
value grows like P , and it is priced. Truncating the spectrum after the leading components,
as a weak-factor or noise reading of the sample suggests, discards priced risk. The omitted
groups lower the attainable Sharpe ratio. Because the maximal Sharpe ratio is finite, the
loss relative to the full collection of limiting groups shrinks to zero as more of those groups
are retained.
   Sections 5.2 and 5.3 thus connect two literatures that are usually read separately. Evi-
dence of premia beyond the leading factors (Lopez-Lira and Roussanov, 2023; Dello-Preite
et al., 2025; Didisheim et al., 2024) and evidence of weak factors (Onatski, 2012; Lettau and
Pelger, 2020a; Giglio et al., 2025) concern the same components: systematic directions with
small growth constants. A finite sample makes them look weak, and a cliff-based model
makes their premia look like near-arbitrage or compensation for diversifiable risk. Without
a cliff, they are strong, systematic, and generically priced, while the maximal Sharpe ratio
stays finite.




                                              38
5.4      Covariance matrix estimation

A test of an asset-pricing model asks whether its factors attain the maximal Sharpe ratio or
whether adding test assets raises it. The GRS test (Gibbons et al., 1989) is the canonical
example: its statistic is a scaled estimate of the increase in maximal squared Sharpe ratio.
In population, that increase is zero when the factors price the test assets. The usual sample
test requires an invertible residual covariance matrix. Related efficiency calculations invert
the return covariance or second-moment matrix.
       Inverting these matrices raises two separate problems. The first is a sample problem. The
sample second-moment matrix becomes ill-conditioned as P approaches the sample length
T and singular when P > T . This dimension-counting problem exists regardless of the
population factor structure and has nothing to do with the absence of a population cliff.
When P > T , the sample second-moment matrix has at most T positive eigenvalues.19
       The second is a population problem. Faced with sample ill-conditioning, researchers
often assume that the population matrix consists of a few pervasive factor directions and a
remainder with bounded eigenvalues. Under the conditions of Theorem 1, managed portfolios
do not admit this decomposition (Lemma A.11).
       The population problem concerns unconditional second moments of managed returns.
Conditional on characteristics, stock returns keep the cliff: K eigenvalues grow like N ,
and the rest stay bounded (Proposition 2). Instrumented principal components (Kelly et
al., 2019) estimate this conditional structure, with stock loadings linear in characteristics.
Principal components of unconditional managed returns target a different object, which has
no cliff under Theorem 1 whatever the factor structure of stocks. Even when stocks have
a single factor with constant loadings, as in (20), nonlinear portfolio weights give managed
portfolios state-dependent exposures, and their spectrum has no cliff (Theorem 4).
  19
    Appendix L gives two finite-sample benchmarks for eigenvalue dispersion and the absence of gaps. The
gap result requires centered returns with independent standardized components; it is not asserted for the
paper’s dependent managed returns.



                                                   39
   Whether a cliff exists matters for covariance estimation, because factor-based estimators
presume one. They represent covariance through a few factor exposures and a separately
modeled remainder: Fan, Fan and Lv (2008) use observed factors, and Fan et al. (2013)
estimate latent factors from leading principal components with their principal orthogonal
complement thresholding (POET) estimator. Conditional stock returns satisfy this pre-
sumption (Proposition 2); unconditional managed returns do not. For managed portfolios,
any fixed principal-component truncation discards eigenvalues that diverge with P . Under
Theorem 5, it also generically discards priced groups and lowers the attainable Sharpe ratio
relative to Ssys . This loss shrinks to zero as more limiting groups are retained, and at a fixed
stock count the limiting groups recover Sfull (Theorem 5). With a growing stock count, the
gap described in Section 5.2 can remain.
   Other methods restrict cross-sectional dependence. The alpha tests of Fan, Liao and
Yao (2015) use sparse residual covariance estimation, and the Sharpe-ratio inference of
Caner, Medeiros and Vasconcelos (2023) uses residual precision estimation in factor models.
Pesaran and Yamagata (2024) allow weak cross-sectional dependence and correct its effect
without estimating an invertible covariance matrix. These restrictions must hold for the
managed returns themselves. Even if individual-stock returns satisfy sparse covariance or
precision restrictions, characteristic-based portfolio weights need not preserve them (Chernov
et al., 2025). Factor truncation and restrictions on cross-sectional dependence both require
that, once finitely many factor directions are removed, the residual second-moment matrix
have a bounded spectrum. Unconditional managed returns fail this requirement: however
many leading components are removed, the largest remaining eigenvalue still grows with the
number of portfolios.
   How much of the total second moment a truncation captures depends on the menu. The
trace of ΨP,N is the menu’s total second moment, summed over portfolios. With the number
of stocks fixed, a growing number of leading components captures almost all of this total



                                               40
for random-feature menus (Appendix E). For refining partitions, any number of components
that is small relative to P captures a vanishing share. Neither fact settles estimation, because
tests and efficient portfolios use the inverse matrix. The inverse weights each direction by
the reciprocal of its eigenvalue. At any finite menu size, the small-eigenvalue directions that
a truncation drops can therefore matter for estimated maximal Sharpe ratios and for the
residual-covariance inverse in the GRS statistic.
    Regularization addresses the inverse without choosing a number of factors. Examples
are ridge regularization, including the prior of Kozak et al. (2020), and the linear and
nonlinear shrinkage estimators of Ledoit and Wolf (2004, 2017, 2020). None of these es-
timators presumes a cliff. The optimality results of Ledoit and Wolf (2017, 2020) do assume
population eigenvalues in a compact subset of (0, ∞), which managed portfolios violate
because their leading eigenvalues grow like P . Regularization makes inversion feasible when
P is comparable to or larger than T , but it biases both the estimated covariance matrix and
the resulting Sharpe-ratio statistics. Valid inference must correct this bias.
    Our companion papers supply those corrections. Chernov et al. (2025) debias the high-
dimensional GRS statistic and restore a valid test of portfolio efficiency when P is comparable
to T . Chernov et al. (2026) similarly correct the bias that high dimensionality and ridge
regularization introduce into estimates of the maximal Sharpe ratio. Those results do not
depend on the theorems in this paper. Our results undercut only regularization that relies
on a cliff: a fixed factor count with a bounded remainder.


6    Conclusion

The eigenvalue cliff, a few diverging eigenvalues followed by a bounded bulk, is the premise
of the approximate factor model and of the empirical methods built on it. Conditioning
information undermines this premise. In modern empirical asset pricing, this information
typically consists of firm characteristics, which set the weights of managed portfolios and,


                                              41
in a conditional factor model, the factor loadings of individual stocks. These loadings also
reflect persistent differences across firms. Within a period, a few factors may then generate
all systematic risk. Across periods, characteristics move, and the weights and loadings
move with them. Model-free empirical work relies on the unconditional second-moment
matrix, which averages over this movement, and the average is an infinite-factor model. For
managed portfolios, this happens whatever the factor structure of stocks. Common variation
in characteristics lets it survive diversification. For individual stocks, it happens generically
when persistent firm differences and common states both take infinitely many values.
   Losing the cliff does not mean losing the absence of arbitrage. As long as conditional
expected returns admit no asymptotic arbitrage, the maximal Sharpe ratio stays finite. What
fails is the APT’s conclusion that finitely many factors price assets. For generic expected
returns, infinitely many groups of principal components earn risk premia. No finite set of
components therefore spans expected returns. Pricing itself does not fail: a single payoff
formed from all returns, possibly as a limit of portfolios, still prices every return. The failure
concerns principal components.
   For empirical work, the components beyond any fixed cutoff are neither noise nor weak
factors. This view reconciles evidence of large premia on small sources of risk with the
evidence on weak factors. Their eigenvalues are small relative to the leading ones but still
grow with the number of assets, and they are priced. Truncating at a fixed number of
components therefore discards priced risk and lowers the attainable Sharpe ratio. Retaining
more groups narrows the loss.       With a growing stock count, however, even all groups
together can fall short of the full maximal Sharpe ratio. Factor-based covariance estimators
presume a cliff. Conditional stock returns have one; unconditional managed returns do not.
Regularization that does not presume a cliff avoids this problem, provided one corrects for
the bias it introduces.

                                            49
A     Mathematical Tools

The appendices follow logical dependence rather than the order of the main text: the proofs
for the Hermite-degree spectrum come after the appendix on risk premia and spanning
because they use its matrix–operator dictionary.
    This appendix collects the operator facts used throughout: Hilbert–Schmidt and trace-
class operators, the min–max principle and Weyl’s inequality, the relation between the
spectra of AB and BA, and an almost-sure lower bound for empirical random-feature kernels.
It closes with the effect of centering on eigenvalues and the normalization of the tradable
discount factor.

Definition A.1 (Hilbert–Schmidt integral operator) The integral operator

                        Z
      (TK f )(z) =          K(z, z̃) f (z̃) dP (z̃)


on L2 (P ) is Hilbert–Schmidt if its kernel is square-integrable, in which case

                   ZZ
      ∥TK ∥2HS =        K(z, z̃)2 dP (z) dP (z̃) = ∥K∥2L2 < ∞.                           (A.1)


Definition A.2 (Trace-class integral operator) The integral operator TK on L2 (P ) is
                                                               P
trace-class if its singular values {si } are summable, ∥TK ∥1 = i si < ∞. For positive self-
                                                                                  P
adjoint TK the singular values coincide with the eigenvalues {λi }, and ∥TK ∥1 = i λi =
tr TK .

Lemma A.4 (Parseval) Let {ek } be an orthonormal basis of a Hilbert space H. Then for
every u ∈ H,

      X
           |⟨ek , u⟩|2 = ∥u∥2 .                                                                  (A.3)
       k


Lemma A.5 (Min–max principle) Let A be a bounded self-adjoint operator on H, a
separable Hilbert space of infinite dimension. Define its Courant–Fischer values, counted
from the top of the spectrum,


      λj (A) :=      sup          inf   ψ, A ψ ,           j = 1, 2, . . .
                     V ⊆H      ψ∈V
                   dim V =j   ∥ψ∥=1



Then for each j exactly one of the following holds.

 (a) λj (A) > sup σess (A), and λj (A) is the j-th largest eigenvalue of A lying above the
      essential spectrum, counted with multiplicity.

 (b) λj (A) = sup σess (A), in which case λk (A) = sup σess (A) for every k ≥ j, and A has at
      most j − 1 eigenvalues above sup σess (A), counted with multiplicity.

In particular,


    λj (A) ≥ sup σess (A)          for every j,      and         λj (A) ↓ sup σess (A) as j → ∞. (A.4)



                                                      51
For a noncompact operator, λj (A) always denotes this Courant–Fischer value, which need
not be an isolated eigenvalue.


Proof of Lemma A.5. This is the min–max principle of Reed and Simon (1978, Theo-
rem XIII.1). That theorem is stated for an operator B bounded below, with the min–
max values µj (B) = supϕ1 ,...,ϕj−1 inf{⟨ψ, Bψ⟩ : ∥ψ∥ = 1, ψ ⊥ ϕ1 , . . . , ϕj−1 } counted from
the bottom of the spectrum, and it gives the dichotomy above with inf σess (B) in place
of sup σess (A). Apply it to B = −A, which is bounded below because A is bounded.
Since σess (−A) = −σess (A), we have inf σess (−A) = − sup σess (A). The spectral theorem
identifies the displayed max–min value with sup{c : dim ran 1(c,∞) (A) ≥ j}: a spectral
subspace above c supplies the lower bound, while every j-dimensional subspace intersects
its orthogonal complement if that spectral subspace has dimension less than j. This gives
µj (−A) = −λj (A) and the stated dichotomy. If case (a) holds at every rank, the decreasing
eigenvalues have finite multiplicity and their limit is a spectral accumulation point, hence
belongs to the essential spectrum. If case (b) occurs, the sequence is already constant
thereafter. Both cases give (A.4).                                                           □


Lemma A.6 (Compressions along increasing subspaces) Let A be a bounded self-
adjoint operator on a separable Hilbert space H. Let V1 ⊆ V2 ⊆ · · · be closed subspaces
                                                              S
of H with orthogonal projections ΠP , let V∞ be the closure of P VP , and let Π∞ be its
orthogonal projection, so that ΠP → Π∞ strongly. For a closed subspace V with projection Π,
write λj (ΠAΠ|V ) for the Courant–Fischer value of Lemma A.5 with the variational subspaces
restricted to V. Then, for every fixed j ≤ dim V∞ ,

                                        
     λj ΠP AΠP |VP       −→ λj Π∞ AΠ∞ |V∞ .


If V∞ = H, the limit is λj (A).



                                              52
Proof of Lemma A.6. Every j-dimensional V ⊆ VP lies in V∞ , so restricting the varia-
tional subspaces gives the upper bound λj (ΠP AΠP |VP ) ≤ λj (Π∞ AΠ∞ |V∞ ). For the lower
bound, fix ε > 0 and a j-dimensional V ⊆ V∞ whose minimum Rayleigh quotient is within
ε of λj (Π∞ AΠ∞ |V∞ ). Strong convergence is uniform on the unit sphere of V , so for P large
the projection ΠP V ⊆ VP is j-dimensional and its Rayleigh quotients converge to those of
V . The argument uses the values computed within VP , which for finite-dimensional VP are
the eigenvalues of a finite matrix; adding a zero orthogonal complement could change them
when they are negative.                                                                    □


Lemma A.7 (Weyl’s inequality) Let A and E be self-adjoint bounded operators on a
Hilbert space H (for example, compact self-adjoint operators and real symmetric matrices),
and let λ1 (·) ≥ λ2 (·) ≥ · · · denote the Courant–Fischer values of Lemma A.5 in decreasing
order (for matrices, see Bhatia, 1997, Corollary III.1.2). For every admissible index k,


      λk (A) + λinf (E) ≤ λk (A + E) ≤ λk (A) + λsup (E),                              (A.5)


where λsup (E) = supx ⟨x,Ex⟩
                       ∥x∥2
                             and λinf (E) = inf x ⟨x,Ex⟩
                                                   ∥x∥2
                                                         . Consequently:

  1. (Perturbation bound) for every k,


            λk (A) − λk (A + E) ≤ ∥E∥op ;                                              (A.6)


  2. (Positive perturbation) if E ⪰ 0, then for every k,


            λk (A) ≤ λk (A + E) ≤ λk (A) + ∥E∥op .                                     (A.7)


Proof of Lemma A.7. By the Courant–Fischer definition, for any bounded self-adjoint T ,




                                               53
     λk (T ) = sup     inf ⟨x, T x⟩.                                                    (A.8)
                V ⊆H    x∈V
              dim V =k ∥x∥=1


For any unit vector x, ⟨x, (A + E)x⟩ = ⟨x, Ax⟩ + ⟨x, Ex⟩ with λinf (E) ≤ ⟨x, Ex⟩ ≤
λsup (E). Substituting the upper bound into the min–max formula, the constant λsup (E)
passes through both the inner inf and outer sup, giving λk (A + E) ≤ λk (A) + λsup (E); the
lower bound is symmetric. This is (A.5). Since ∥E∥op = max(|λsup (E)|, |λinf (E)|), applying
(A.5) to A + E and to (A + E) + (−E) = A yields |λk (A) − λk (A + E)| ≤ ∥E∥op , which is
1. If E ⪰ 0 then λinf (E) ≥ 0 and λsup (E) = ∥E∥op , and (A.5) reduces to 2.                □


Lemma A.8 Let A : H2 → H1 and B : H1 → H2 be bounded linear operators between
Hilbert spaces (in particular, A = M an m × n matrix and B = M ′ its transpose). Then AB
(on H1 ) and BA (on H2 ) have the same nonzero spectrum. Their generalized eigenspaces
at each nonzero eigenvalue have equal dimension. An isolated nonzero eigenvalue of finite
algebraic multiplicity for either product has the same algebraic and geometric multiplicities
for the other. If B = A∗ , the restrictions of AA∗ and A∗ A to their kernel complements are
unitarily equivalent; their ordered variational values coincide, with zeros appended in finite
dimension.


Proof of Lemma A.8. Equality of the nonzero spectra follows from the resolvent identity


     (I − zBA)−1 = I + zB(I − zAB)−1 A


whenever the inverse on the right exists, and the same identity with A, B interchanged. For
multiplicity, fix a nonzero eigenvalue λ and n ≥ 1. The intertwining identity B(AB − λI)n =
(BA − λI)n B maps ker(AB − λI)n into ker(BA − λI)n . It is injective there: if Bv = 0,
then (AB − λI)n v = (−λ)n v, so membership in the kernel forces v = 0. Interchanging A


                                             54
and B gives the reverse dimension inequality. Thus the generalized eigenspaces have the
same dimension for every n. If λ is isolated, the same intertwining identity holds for the
resolvents and hence for their Riesz projections. The map B is an isomorphism between
the corresponding Riesz subspaces: its inverse is A(BA)−1 restricted to the latter subspace,
where BA is invertible because λ ̸= 0. This transfers finite algebraic multiplicity and proves
equality; n = 1 gives geometric multiplicity. Finally, polar decomposition A = U |A| gives
AA∗ = U (A∗ A)U ∗ , with U unitary between the kernel complements. Positivity and zero
padding give equality of all ordered variational values.                                           □
