Lemma A.11 (Centering and pervasive factor loadings) Let Y ∈ Rn have finite sec-
ond moments, ΨY = E[Y Y ′ ], µ = E[Y ], and V = Var(Y ). Then, for 1 ≤ j < n,


     λj+1 (ΨY ) ≤ λj (V ) ≤ λj (ΨY ).                                                  (A.16)


For a sequence with dimension n → ∞, if lim inf n λj (ΨY,n )/n > 0 for every fixed j, then

               λj (Vn )           λj+1 (ΨY,n )
     lim inf            ≥ lim inf              > 0.                                    (A.17)
      n→∞         n         n→∞        n

The growth constants need not agree before and after centering. For a covariance decom-
                  ′
position VN = BN BN + EN , with BN having K columns and 0 ⪯ EN ⪯ CIN , one has
                                    ′
λK+1 (VN ) ≤ C. If in addition λK (BN BN ) → ∞, exactly K eigenvalues diverge.


Proof of Lemma A.11. The identity ΨY = V + µµ′ and rank-one interlacing give (A.16).
Dividing by the cross-sectional size transfers positive fixed-index lower limits from ΨY to
                    ′
V . The rank of BN BN is at most K, so Weyl’s inequality bounds λK+1 (VN ) by C; its

                                                  58
                                                ′                     ′
K potentially nonzero eigenvalues are those of BN BN . Since VN ⪰ BN BN , the additional
loading condition makes all K diverge. Merely writing a K-factor representation does not
give this condition: BN = (1, 0, . . . , 0)′ and EN = IN produce eigenvalues 2, 1, . . . , 1.   □


Remark A.12 (Normalization of the tradable discount factor) Given Xt and excess
                                                       ′
returns rt+1 , write µ = E[rt+1 | Xt ] and Σ = E[rt+1 rt+1 | Xt ] ≻ 0. Within the normalized
                          ′
affine family mt+1 = 1 − rt+1 aSDF , the unique coefficient satisfying E[mt+1 rt+1 | Xt ] = 0
is aSDF = Σ−1 µ. It also uniquely minimizes E[(1 − rt+1
                                                    ′
                                                        aSDF )2 | Xt ] over that family. If
V = Σ − µµ′ ≻ 0 and SR2cond = µ′ V −1 µ, then


           ′             1 − (rt+1 − µ)′ V −1 µ
      1 − rt+1 Σ−1 µ =                          .
                              1 + SR2cond

Thus the second-moment and covariance formulas give proportional tradable pricing kernels
under two normalizations: unit intercept and unit conditional mean. Orthogonality to excess
returns alone does not fix their scale or establish pointwise positivity. The unconditional
formulas follow by dropping the conditioning.


B     Stock Assumptions and Unconditional Factor Counts

B.1    Common states and fixed-effect-dependent laws

Assumption 3 uses a common conditional characteristic law across stocks. We first allow this
law to depend on fixed effects, then explain the exchangeability representation behind the
common-state formulation. The following assumption replaces only the common-law clause
of Assumption 3; its nonrandom fixed-effect array, conditional factor model, conditional
moments, and residual conditions remain in force. The stock results require regularity of the
fixed-effect array and its responses. The characteristic-law extension itself imposes only the
conditional independence stated here.



                                                    59
Assumption B.1 (Fixed-effect characteristic laws) Each period, conditional on the
common state Wt and the nonrandom fixed effects, characteristics are independent across
stocks, with


      Xi,t ∼ p(· | Si , Wt ).                                                                  (B.1)


Here (s, w) 7→ p(· | s, w) is a Borel probability kernel on the characteristic space. The
baseline in Assumption 3 is the special case p(· | s, w) = p0 (· | w) for every s, where p0
denotes the common conditional characteristic law.

   In this extension a fixed effect can change both the loading B(x, s) and the conditional
distribution of characteristics. With finitely many fixed effects, the model is a collection of
conditionally independent fixed-effect classes, each with its own conditional characteristic
law. With infinitely many fixed effects, the formulation also allows every stock to have a
different fixed effect; no repeated observations of an individual fixed effect are required.

Theorem B.2 (de Finetti–Hewitt–Savage representation) Let (Xi )i≥1 be an infinite
exchangeable sequence of random variables taking values in a Polish space S (in our applica-
tion S = Rd ); that is, for every n the law of (X1 , . . . , Xn ) is invariant under all permutations
of the indices. Then there exists a random probability measure µ on S, the directing random
measure, such that, conditionally on µ, the Xi are i.i.d. with common law µ. Taking W = µ
gives a probability kernel w 7→ p(· | w) such that, conditionally on W , the Xi are i.i.d. with
law p(· | W ). Writing PW for the law of W = µ, the marginal law of each Xi is

                Z
      p(dx) =       p(dx | w) PW (dw).


The sequence is i.i.d. if and only if its directing measure µ is almost surely nonrandom.
When W = µ, this is equivalent to a degenerate law for W .



                                                 60
   For S = {0, 1} this is de Finetti’s original theorem (de Finetti, 1937); the extension to
Polish spaces is due to Hewitt and Savage (1955). In the baseline of Assumption 3, the the-
orem applies to the entire characteristic sequence at each date. For finitely many types, the
corresponding partially exchangeable representation (Kallenberg, 2005) requires invariance of
the joint law under separate permutations within each infinite fixed-effect class. It represents
the classes as independent conditional on their collection of directing measures, which can
be included in a single common state Wt . Under Assumption B.1, conditional independence
is imposed directly. With infinitely many fixed effects, and particularly when fixed effects
occur only once, within-class exchangeability alone does not yield that assumption.
   For M < ∞ types with positive limiting shares πℓ , the conditional strong law within each
fixed-effect class and convergence of the shares imply that the pooled empirical characteristic
                                  PM
law converges almost surely to       ℓ=1 πℓ p(· | sℓ , Wt ). More generally define the limiting-

population conditional and marginal laws by

                    Z                                       Z
      p(· | w) :=        p(· | s, w) ν(ds),       p(·) :=       p(· | w) PW (dw).
                     T

     s and PW -almost every w.

   Throughout the appendices, the nonrandom array S is a suppressed parameter: ΣN (Xt ),
µG (Xt ) and Ω(Xt ) stand for ΣN (Xt ; S), µG (Xt ; S) and Ω(Xt ; S). Because S is nonrandom,
σ(Xt , S1:N ) = σ(Xt ), so conditioning on Xt alone gives the same moments.



                                                     62
   For the limiting population, sample two fixed effects independently from ν, then sample
their characteristics independently conditional on these fixed effects and W . Their charac-
teristic pair law is

                       Z                                               Z
      P2 (dz, du) =        p(dz | w) p(du | w) PW (dw),   p(· | w) =       p(· | s, w) ν(ds).   (B.6)


   The random fixed effects in this construction describe population sampling; the actual
array (Si ) remains nonrandom. With finitely many types and convergent shares, (B.6) is
also the limit of the law of a uniformly sampled ordered pair of distinct stocks. At finite
N , sampling without replacement generally gives a different pair law. In the baseline, the
formula holds for any two distinct stocks. If the pooled directing law p(· | W ) is nonrandom,
then P2 = p ⊗ p. This last statement concerns the pooled population; in the extension it
does not assert that stocks with different fixed effects have identical characteristic laws.


Sufficient conditions for Assumption B.3(i). With finitely many types and convergent
shares, conditional independence and the bounded loadings yield (B.2) by the strong law
within each type. For a general regular fixed-effect array, a sufficient additional condition is
that s 7→ B(x, s)B(x, s)′ p(dx | s, w) be bounded and continuous for PW -almost every w.
          R

Conditional independence and boundedness make the centered empirical average converge
almost surely to zero, while (B.5) gives convergence of its conditional mean. Without this
condition or another suitable law of large numbers, weak convergence of the fixed-effect
array alone implies neither (B.2) nor convergence of the characteristic empirical law to the
mixture p(· | w); the limit therefore remains the explicit requirement in Assumption B.3(i).
Its nonsingularity is a requirement on ΣB (w) for PW -almost every w: in almost every state, no
nonzero a ∈ RK has a′ B(x, s) = 0 for ν(ds) p(dx | s, w)-almost every (s, x). Averaging over
                B(x, s)B(x, s)′ p(dx | s) ν(ds) nonsingular as well. The converse fails, which

is why the conditional form is the one imposed. Here p(dx | s) = p(dx | s, w) PW (dw).

B.3   Response configurations and genericity

Definition B.5 (Response configurations and genericity) Fix T , W, ν, PW as in As-
sumption B.3(v), K ≥ 1, a measurable factor second moment Ω(w) ≻ 0 for PW -almost
every w with CΩ := ess supw ∥Ω(w)∥ < ∞, and a constant L > 0. The space of response
configurations is


      CL := {b ∈ Cb (T × W; RK ) : ∥b∥∞ ≤ L},         d(b, eb) := ∥b − eb∥∞ .                (B.10)

                                                              R
A stock model has configuration b when, for every s ∈ T ,         B(x, s) p(dx | s, w) = b(s, w) for
PW -almost every w. The requirement applies to every sampled fixed effect, including those
with zero ν-mass. Thus the laws of fixed effects and states, and the factor second moments,
are held fixed; the conditional mean responses vary. The space CL is a closed subset of a
Banach space and hence a complete metric space. A property is generic here if it holds on a
residual subset, meaning that its exceptions form a countable union of nowhere dense sets.


                                               65
    The count is governed by a single object. For a fixed effect s and state w, let B̄s (w) =
         R
b(s, w) = B(x, s) p(dx | s, w) be the expected loading of a stock with fixed effect s in state
w. Let


      φs := Ω(·)1/2 B̄s (·) ∈ HW := L2 (PW ; RK )                                         (B.11)


be its state-response profile: the vector-valued function of the state that records how a fixed-
effect-s stock’s exposure moves as the state moves. The count is set by the spread of these
profiles across fixed effects, carried by the fixed-effect overlap operator on HW ,

      O := ES∼ν φS ⊗ φS         =        φs ⊗ φs ν(ds),                                   (B.12)

      b(s, w)′ Ω(w)b(s, w) ≤ E[B(X, s)′ Ω(w)B(X, s) | s, w] ≤ q̄.                         (B.13)

   Simultaneous genericity. Baire’s theorem on the complete space CL makes the count-
able intersection in (B.17) dense. Its complement is a countable union of relatively closed
sets with empty interior, proving residuality. If M is finite, dim L2 (ν) = M bounds the rank
by M , so that intersection equals UM . If M = ∞, positive eigenvalues at every finite index
are equivalent to infinite rank.                                                                        □



                                                          68
Lemma B.7 (Common-law realization) Every configuration in (B.10) is realized by a
bounded stock model satisfying Assumptions 3 and B.3. The characteristic law can be held
fixed across all configurations.


Proof of Lemma B.7. Since W is Polish, choose a Borel injection ι : W → [0, 1] with Borel
inverse on its image. Take independent vectors ηi ∈ {−1, 1}K with independent symmetric
coordinates, independent of W , and put


      A = Ω(W ),       Xi = (ι(W ), ηi , A),       B(Xi , s) = b(s, W ) + ηi .


Choose a version of Ω taking values in the bounded set {A ⪰ 0 : ∥A∥ ≤ CΩ } everywhere. The
characteristic domain is ι(W) × {−1, 1}K × {A ⪰ 0 : ∥A∥ ≤ CΩ }, a Borel subset of a finite-
dimensional Euclidean space after vectorizing the matrix coordinate. On this characteristic
domain define B((z, e, A), s) = b(s, ι−1 (z)) + e, and extend it boundedly and measurably
                                                              √
outside the domain if necessary. Its norm is at most L + K. Conditional on W , the
Xi are i.i.d., and their law is the same for all fixed effects and all configurations b. Only
the loading function varies with the configuration. Take G = A1/2 ζ, where ζ is independent,
mean zero, and has covariance IK , and add independent bounded residuals of a fixed positive
variance. Since the matrix coordinate A is observed in every Xi , the conditional factor mean
is zero and its second moment is exactly A. The latter is the empirical average of the matrix
coordinate, a bounded continuous functional of the empirical characteristic law. Moreover,
E[B(Xi , s) | W ] = b(s, W ) for every s.
   For each fixed w, the map s 7→ b(s, w)b(s, w)′ is bounded and continuous. Writing
Yi = B(Xi , Si ) = b(Si , W ) + ηi , equation (B.5) and the strong law for independent bounded
variables, applied conditionally on W , give

          N                  Z
      1 X
            Yi Yi′ −→ IK +         b(s, W )b(s, W )′ ν(ds) ≻ 0    almost surely.
      N i=1


                                                  69
Thus Assumption B.3(i) also holds, even at configurations with zero conditional mean
responses. This construction shows that the configuration space contains realizable stock
models throughout, including all the perturbations used in the density proof.
                                                                                            □

   By Lemma B.7, every configuration in (B.10), including each perturbed configuration
in the proof of Proposition B.6, is realized by an admissible stock model. The genericity
statement is relative to the specified response space and uniform topology. It does not assert
genericity in every restricted parametric family of characteristic laws and loadings. For
example, if all b(s, w) are constant in w and Ω is constant, the rank is at most K. More
generally, if the state law has only J < ∞ support points, then rank Ob ≤ KJ. Infinite state
variation is therefore necessary for the infinite-rank conclusion.

C     Standard Menu Constructions and Conditions

This appendix restates the menu conditions of Assumption 1 and describes the standard
menu constructions: random features, a fixed basis, and partitions, each with the conditions
it needs. Later appendices verify Assumption 1 for each construction.


C.1    Formal menu conditions

Definition C.1 (Formal menu conditions) For reference, we restate Assumption 1. At
every fixed N , let Rk,t+1 denote the managed return from menu f (P ) , as in (2); the menus
need not be nested. All limits and suprema in P are taken along one specified increasing
sequence of menu sizes. For weighting functions with random labels drawn independently
of the panel, the conditions hold almost surely conditional on the label sequence. Every
Rk,t+1 is square integrable. There is a constant Cmenu < ∞, the same for every P and every
arbitrary managed portfolio, such that for every measurable ϕ with square-integrable return

C.2   Types of weight menus

Empirical asset pricing builds managed portfolios in two main ways: from parameterized
weight functions, or from partitions of the characteristic space. We use three such menus
by name: random-feature menus and fixed-basis menus, both built from parameterized
weight functions, and partition menus. The three subsections below state their conditions.
Throughout this subsection, p = N −1 i≤N law(Xi,t ) is the pooled characteristic law; µθ
                                      P

denotes a distribution of portfolio labels.


Parameterized weight functions. The first approach specifies a family of weight func-
tions f : Rd × Θ → R. A menu of P portfolios is then indexed by labels θ1 , . . . , θP ∈ Θ,
       (P )
with fk       = f (·; θk ). The labels may be drawn randomly from a distribution µθ (dθ) on Θ,
as in Didisheim et al. (2024); fixed in advance, as in Chen, Roussanov and Wang (2022);
or estimated from the data, as in Kelly et al. (2026). The random-feature and fixed-basis
conditions here concern labels chosen independently of returns; menus whose labels are
estimated from the same returns fall outside them.
   The first, the random-feature menu, consists of random Fourier features,

                 √
      fk (x) =    2 cos(ωk′ x + bk ),      θk = (ωk , bk ),




                                                     75
whose weight functions do not depend on P at all; only the number of draws does. The
second, the fixed-basis menu, is


          (P )
         fk (x) = P 1/2 qk Hk (x),


where {Hk } is an orthonormal basis of L2 (p) and the coefficients qk are bounded. Examples
include Hermite polynomials for a Gaussian p, or a complete spline system with refining
knots, orthonormalized in L2 (p).


Sorts, partitions, and trees. The second approach partitions the characteristic space
into cells C1 , . . . , CP . Portfolio k holds equally weighted stocks whose characteristics fall in
Ck :


          (P )
         fk (x) = P 1{x ∈ Ck }.


For d = 1, characteristic-quantile cells give an expected-count version of a univariate sort.
For d > 1, they give double, triple, and more general multivariate sorts.20
       Cattaneo et al. (2020) develop estimation and inference for characteristic-sorted portfo-
lios, including the choice of the number of portfolios. Cattaneo et al. (2026) study beta-sorted
portfolios, where the sorting variables are factor exposures estimated in a first step.
       Partition menus also encompass tree-based procedures. A decision tree recursively splits
Rd into cells, and its leaves define the portfolios. Adding a split refines the menu by
dividing one existing cell into two. Thus tree leaves form partition menus. The partition
conditions below cover trees whose splits are fixed in advance and whose levels satisfy the
  20
    The weight P 1{x ∈ Ck } normalizes by expected cell size,Pp(Ck ) ≈ 1/P , rather than by the realized
number of stocks in the cell. The usual empirical sort, n−1  k,t  i∈Ck ri,t+1 , is not exactly of the managed
form in (2), since nk,t depends on the entire cross-section rather than on stock i’s own characteristics. The
two do not coincide exactly in expectation because the realized reciprocal cell count is nonlinear. They
can become asymptotically equivalent in a joint limit in which expected cell counts diverge and concentrate
uniformly. That is not the fixed-N , growing-P limit studied here: expected cell counts then tend to zero.
The expected-count version belongs to (2) and remains well defined even when a cell is empty.


                                                     76
mass conditions. Moritz and Zimmermann (2016) construct conditional portfolio sorts using
trees; Bryzgalova et al. (2025) use tree leaves to form cross-sections of test assets; and Cong
et al. (2025) grow trees to expand the efficient frontier.
   A growing menu must settle and remain asymptotically complete. Conditions (C.2)–
(C.3) of Definition C.1 make its co-moment operators converge on each managed return,
with one bound valid for every arbitrary managed portfolio. Asymptotic completeness,
condition (C.4), ensures that the limit detects every nonzero arbitrary managed portfolio.
Random features achieve it through the label distribution, a fixed basis exhausts L2 (p), and
generating partitions resolve every measurable characteristic weighting.

    Linear independence of every finite menu does not by itself guarantee the final richness condition. For

D        Managed-Payoff Operators and the General Lower Bound

This appendix defines the operators and settled forms that a growing menu generates, proves
the lower bound in Theorem 1, and states a formal common-shock condition that is sufficient
for Assumption 2.

Remark D.1 (Single-stock and panel weightings) Condition (C.2) checks the menu
against portfolios built the way the menu is, through a weighting ψ of a single stock’s
characteristics.    Widening it to an arbitrary η : RN ×d → RN , with portfolio return
1 ′
 r η(Xt ),
N t+1
              can make (C.4) fail even with two stocks. Suppose for this paragraph that
the characteristics are identically distributed, so that no per-stock reweighting intervenes.
Then ⟨Rk , N1 r′ η(X)⟩ = N12 i E[fk (Xi,t ) ui (Xt )] with u = ΣN (Xt )η(Xt ), so Aη depends on
                            P


                                                 81
                                                    P
η only through the one-coordinate marginal z 7→         i E[ui (Xt ) | Xi,t   = z]. With N = 2,
Σ2 = I2 and exchangeable characteristics, the constant panel weighting η = (1, −1)′ yields
a nonzero payoff orthogonal to every menu return: its second moment is 1/2, whereas
E[fk (X1 ) − fk (X2 )] = 0. Thus its settled limit Aη vanishes for every menu. The restriction
to ψ matches the construction in (2): the menu’s own weights are functions of one stock’s
characteristics, so the arbitrary managed portfolios are drawn from the same class as the
menu returns.

   Restricting attention to D costs nothing and is what makes the condition reachable
from Assumption 1. It costs nothing because every Rk lies in D, so AP maps D into itself
and annihilates D⊥ ; its nonzero eigenvalues are therefore those of its compression to D, and
enlarging the supremum in (D.4) beyond D could only add subspaces on which q vanishes. It
is what makes the condition reachable because (C.2) is a statement about arbitrary managed
portfolios and says nothing outside D. Remark D.1 explains why D is built from weightings
of a single stock’s characteristics. The lower-bound argument works on finite-dimensional
subspaces of D, so it needs no uniform operator bound: on each such subspace, bilinearity
upgrades pointwise settling to uniform convergence. The bound in (C.3) is imposed on
AP and passes to its strong limit, with the same constant Cmenu uniformly over arbitrary
managed portfolios and P , and also over N in the growing-stock case. It bounds the size
of spectral mass, but does not prevent a bounded amount from moving through directions
that change with P .
   The empirical co-moment kernel and its stronger convergence condition are

   Condition (D.8) is equivalent to dim ess spanw range(Qw ) = ∞, the essential closed span
being the smallest closed subspace containing range(Qw ) for PW -almost every w, which is
unaffected by the versions chosen on a null set. Indeed, positivity gives ker Q∞ = {f :
QW f = 0 almost surely}, and taking orthogonal complements gives the equivalence.
The common-state rank condition describes the limiting economy. For fixed complete menu
geometries it also determines the rank of the limiting portfolio spectrum: the large-stock
limits for random-feature and partition menus identify the number of positive growth con-
stants with this rank. It is separate from topological genericity: an infinite-rank condition
does not itself establish genericity on an unspecified space of models.
   Assumption D.2 is sufficient for Assumption 2: the positive operator Q∞ has arbitrarily
large finite-dimensional subspaces on which its quadratic form is positive definite, and (D.7)
identifies that form with the limiting return second moments. The uniformity over the full
unit ball in (D.7) is stronger than the pointwise convergence needed in Assumption 2. Infinite
support of Wt alone does not imply (D.8): infinitely many states may generate the same
conditional law and hence the same Qw .

Lemma D.4 (Rich characteristics make D infinite-dimensional) If ΣN (Xt ) is posi-
tive definite almost surely, E[tr ΣN (Xt )] < ∞, and the pooled characteristic law p has infinite
support (equivalently, the fixed-N panel Xt has infinite support), then dim D = ∞.


Proof of Lemma D.4. Let Sb ⊂ L2 (p) be the vector space of simple functions. The
moment assumption makes JN ψ := Φψ square integrable for every ψ ∈ Sb , because


       E[Φ2ψ ] ≤ N −2 E[∥ψN (Xt )∥2 tr ΣN (Xt )] ≤ N −1 ∥ψ∥2∞ E[tr ΣN (Xt )] < ∞.


Thus JN Sb ⊂ D by (D.1). If JN ψ = 0 in L2 , then the law of iterated expectations and (1)
give

                       1
       0 = E[Φ2ψ ] =      E[ψN (Xt )′ ΣN (Xt )ψN (Xt )] .
                       N2

The integrand is nonnegative, and ΣN (Xt ) is positive definite almost surely by the non-
degeneracy imposed after (1). Hence ψ(Xi,t ) = 0 almost surely for every i. Since p =


                                                   85
N −1                                                  2
       P
        i law(Xi,t ), this is equivalent to ψ = 0 in L (p).   Thus JN is injective on Sb . Infinite
support of p supplies arbitrarily many disjoint positive-mass sets, whose indicators are
linearly independent; hence dim Sb = ∞ and therefore dim D = ∞. We neither define
nor need JN on all of L2 (p): that stronger assertion would require an additional operator-
domain bound. Notice that asymptotic completeness is not used in this dimension argument;
it is a separate condition on the settled form.                                                  □


Remark D.5 (The managed-payoff space of one stock) For N = 1, define the mea-
                  2
sure νr (dx) = E[rt+1 | Xt = x]PX (dx). It is σ-finite. The map f 7→ f (Xt )rt+1 satisfies

                              Z
                      2
       E[(f (Xt )rt+1 ) ] =       f (x)2 νr (dx).


It is therefore an isometry from L2 (νr ) onto the managed-payoff space, which has infinite
dimension exactly when νr has infinite support. Under the standing positive conditional
second moment, νr and PX have the same null sets, so infinite support of characteristics
suffices. Lemma D.4 gives the corresponding result for multiple stocks.

The infinite-support hypothesis of Lemma D.4 is not a restriction on the menu and cannot
be dropped: if the characteristics take finitely many values then L2 (p) is finite-dimensional,
D is too, and αj = 0 for every j beyond dim D, whatever the menu. The retained-exposure
condition (C.4) does not itself exclude this degeneracy, so Assumption 1 explicitly requires
arbitrarily many independent square-integrable managed returns. Lemma D.4 supplies a
sufficient condition for this requirement; Theorem 1 imposes payoff variety directly and does
not require E[tr ΣN (Xt )] < ∞.

Lemma D.6 (Completeness and positive lower bounds) Under (D.3), αj > 0 for ev-
ery j exactly when the settled form q has infinite rank. If dim D = ∞, strong settling (C.2)
and asymptotic completeness (C.4) imply this infinite-rank condition.



                                                    86
Proof of Lemma D.6. Each qP is symmetric and positive semidefinite, so q is too, being
its pointwise limit.
   The first assertion uses only (D.3); the second needs the representation of q that (C.2)
supplies, since (C.4) is a statement about AΦ .
   Asymptotic completeness is positive definiteness of q. Cauchy–Schwarz for a
positive semidefinite form gives q(Φ, Φ′ )2 ≤ q(Φ, Φ) q(Φ′ , Φ′ ), so q(Φ, Φ) = 0 forces ⟨AΦ , Φ′ ⟩ =
q(Φ, Φ′ ) = 0 for every Φ′ ∈ D. Each AP Φ is a finite combination of the Rk and so lies in
D; hence AΦ lies in the closure of D, and by continuity the display extends from D to that
closure, giving ∥AΦ ∥2 = 0. Conversely AΦ = 0 gives q(Φ, Φ) = 0. So (C.4) says exactly that
q(Φ, Φ) > 0 for every nonzero Φ ∈ D.
   Infinite rank is what positivity needs. Write ker q = {Φ ∈ D : q(Φ, Φ) = 0}, a
subspace by the Cauchy–Schwarz step, and rank q := dim(D/ ker q). If rank q ≥ j, choose
Φ1 , . . . , Φj ∈ D independent modulo ker q and set V = span{Φ1 , . . . , Φj }, so that V ∩ ker q =
{0}. On V the map Φ 7→ q(Φ, Φ) is a quadratic form on a finite-dimensional space, hence
continuous, and strictly positive off the origin; its minimum over the compact unit sphere of
V is attained and strictly positive, so αj ≥ minΦ∈V,∥Φ∥=1 q(Φ, Φ) > 0 by (D.4). Conversely, if
rank q = m < ∞ then every V of dimension j > m meets ker q nontrivially, the minimum in
(D.4) is zero for every such V , and αj = 0. So αj > 0 for every j exactly when rank q = ∞.
   The dimension hypothesis. Completeness makes ker q = {0}, so rank q = dim D = ∞
under the stated hypothesis. Lemma D.4 supplies infinite dimension from infinite character-
istic support.                                                                                    □

sphere {ϕ ∈ Vj : G∞ (ϕ, ϕ) = 1}: by Cauchy–Schwarz for a positive form,

Suppose their minima on this sphere were not bounded away from zero. There would then
be Nℓ → ∞ and sphere points ϕℓ → ϕ∗ such that bNℓ (ϕℓ , ϕℓ ) → 0. Equicontinuity would
imply bNℓ (ϕ∗ , ϕ∗ ) → 0. But GN (ϕ∗ , ϕ∗ ) → 1, and Condition (iii) of Assumption 2 gives
lim inf N bN (ϕ∗ , ϕ∗ )/GN (ϕ∗ , ϕ∗ ) > 0, a contradiction. Thus bN (ϕ, ϕ) ≥ δj G∞ (ϕ, ϕ) eventually
for some δj > 0. Taking cj = δj /2 proves (D.11), including its strict positivity.                □


   The bound (D.11) is therefore a consequence of the stated main-text assumptions. The
economy supplies nondegenerate limiting returns; pointwise retained exposure and the uni-
form menu bound then prevent an increasingly weak combination within any fixed finite
collection. Weightings with uniformly bounded second moments form a linear class, so no
separate admissibility condition on their combinations is needed.

Consequently,

                                         λj (ΨP,N )
     0 < cj ≤ λ∗j := lim inf lim inf                ≤ Cmenu .
                         N →∞       P →∞     P

The sequence λ∗j is nonincreasing because every finite-menu spectrum is ordered. This proves
(10). Lemma D.8 derives the bound from the economy’s limiting payoff variety and the
menu’s pointwise exposure.                                                                         □


   The growth of each eigenvalue at a fixed position follows from retained exposure to fixed
                                                   ϕ
arbitrary managed portfolios. For a fixed nonzero Rt+1 , the settling and retained-exposure
conditions give

           P
     1 X                    ϕ
           Rk,t+1 E[Rk,t+1 Rt+1 ] −→ Aϕ                  in L2 ,             Aϕ ̸= 0.          (D.12)
     P k=1

The connection to ΨP can be seen by taking a combination of the current menu returns:

     P
     X              h        P
                             X                  i       P
                                                        X
           Rk,t+1 E Rk,t+1          al Rl,t+1       =         Rk,t+1 ãk ,        ã = ΨP a.   (D.13)
     k=1                     l=1                        k=1



                                                          90
The menu’s response to this combination is recorded by the second-moment matrix. To
obtain a bound for the eigenvalue at position j, the proof starts from j independent arbitrary
managed portfolios held fixed as P grows. The menu retains every combination of those
returns, which gives a positive lower bound at that position. Infinite characteristic variety
lets us repeat the argument for any fixed j. This does not say that all P eigenvalues are
large at once; the size of the bound can decline with j. Section 5.3 develops that distinction.
    The bound (D.10) is one-sided. For an orthonormal sequence eP , take AP = eP ⟨eP , ·⟩.
Then λ1 (AP ) = 1 for every P , whereas AP Φ → 0 for every fixed Φ. Thus even strong settling
alone need not preserve spectral values. Kernel convergence (D.6) rules this out: it implies
convergence in Hilbert–Schmidt norm and hence in operator norm. Lemma A.7 then gives
λj (AP ) → λj (TC ) for every fixed j.


E     Portfolio Kernels and Spectral Limits

This appendix represents a random-feature menu by its portfolio kernel. It shows that the
lower bound of Theorem 1 survives at fixed N under a Hilbert–Schmidt condition weaker
than the integrability condition (C.5), and it reduces the kernel spectrum to an eigenvalue
problem in characteristics.

Step 3: ordering controls the Hilbert–Schmidt norm. If 0 ⪯ A ⪯ B are Hilbert–
                         
Schmidt, then tr A(B − A) ≥ 0. To see this, note first that a product of two Hilbert–

                                                                               P
C := B − A ⪰ 0, which is Hilbert–Schmidt hence compact, diagonalize C = k µk ek ⊗ ek
                                                        P                P
with µk ≥ 0 and {ek } orthonormal; then tr(AC) = k ⟨ek , ACek ⟩ = k µk ⟨ek , Aek ⟩ ≥ 0

E[Kn2 ] ≤ E[K 2 ].

Theorem E.5 (The eigenvalue cliff fails at finite N : extension) Let N be fixed, let
θk be i.i.d. with law µθ and independent of the panel, and set fk = f (·; θk ). Suppose

   Every fixed-index eigenvalue therefore diverges, so a fixed factor count cannot leave a
uniformly bounded residual spectrum.

E.2    From portfolios to characteristics

We restrict TK to the economically relevant invariant subspace of functions linear in returns,
Φ(Z) = r′ η(X) with η : RN ×d → RN , and integrate the returns out. The restriction discards
nothing: every eigenfunction of TK with a nonzero eigenvalue is linear in r, as the proof of
                                                    ′
the proposition below shows. Write ΣN (X) = E[rt+1 rt+1 | Xt = X]. For this subsection
assume the two-sided bounds σ 2 IN ⪯ ΣN (X) ⪯ Σ̄N IN , which hold by (B.8) in the factor
model, and define the symmetrized matrix kernel


      k̃(X, X̃) = ΣN (X)1/2 k(X, X̃) ΣN (X̃)1/2 .                                      (E.19)


Let (r̃, X̃) be an independent copy of (r, X). Using E[r̃ r̃′ | X̃] = ΣN (X̃), the eigenvalue
equation TK Φ = λΦ reduces, with the factor N12 inherited from the portfolio kernel (E.3), to



           Z

      N2
               k(X, X̃) ΣN (X̃) η(X̃) PN (dX̃) = λ η(X) .                              (E.20)


Equivalently, in terms of the symmetrized weighting ϕ̃(X) = ΣN (X)1/2 η(X), the eigenvalue
equation (E.20) becomes the symmetric problem

Proposition E.6 At fixed N , suppose Assumption E.1 holds and σ 2 IN ⪯ ΣN (X) ⪯ Σ̄N IN
for PN -almost every X, with 0 < σ 2 ≤ Σ̄N < ∞. The nonzero eigenvalues of the portfolio
kernel operator (E.4) (restricted to the invariant subspace of functions linear in r) coincide
with the nonzero eigenvalues of the symmetric matrix kernel operator

                           Z
                      1
     (T̃ ϕ̃)(X) =     N2
                               k̃(X, X̃) ϕ̃(X̃) PN (dX̃)                                 (E.22)


acting on L2 (PN ; RN ), the space of square-integrable functions ϕ̃ : RN ×d → RN with

     Z
         ∥ϕ̃(X)∥2 PN (dX) < ∞.


   The map η 7→ ΣN (X)1/2 η is a similarity transform: it preserves eigenvalues while making
the operator self-adjoint, so the spectrum can be read off a problem in the N characteristics
alone.


Proof of Proposition E.6. The map Lη = r′ η(X) satisfies


     σ 2 ∥η∥2L2 (PN ) ≤ ∥Lη∥2L2 (P) ≤ Σ̄N ∥η∥2L2 (PN ) .


Its range is therefore closed. The kernel representation implies that TK annihilates its
orthogonal complement, so self-adjointness puts the range of TK inside this closed linear-
return space. In particular, every eigenfunction with a nonzero eigenvalue has the form
r′ η(X) with η ∈ L2 (PN ; RN ). Equation (E.9) makes TK and T̃ Hilbert–Schmidt under the
stated condition.
   Let λ ̸= 0 be an eigenvalue of TK with eigenfunction Φ(Z) = r′ η(X), so that η solves



                                                     101
(E.20). Multiplying (E.20) on the left by ΣN (X)1/2 gives

         Z
    1
             ΣN (X)1/2 k(X, X̃) ΣN (X̃)1/2 ΣN (X̃)1/2 η(X̃) PN (dX̃) = λ ΣN (X)1/2 η(X) . (E.23)
                                                          
    N2



With ϕ̃(X) = ΣN (X)1/2 η(X) and k̃ as in (E.19), this is exactly T̃ ϕ̃ = λϕ̃. Conversely, if
T̃ ϕ̃ = λϕ̃ with λ ̸= 0, then η = ΣN (X)−1/2 ϕ̃ solves (E.20). Here almost-sure invertibility of
ΣN (X) would not suffice: for η 7→ ΣN (X)1/2 η to map L2 (PN ; RN ) onto itself, with an inverse
that also maps it into itself, both ΣN (X)1/2 and ΣN (X)−1/2 must be essentially bounded.
This is exactly the uniform ellipticity assumed in the proposition: for PN -a.e. X,


                                                1/2
         σ ∥η(X)∥ ≤ ∥ΣN (X)1/2 η(X)∥ ≤ Σ̄N ∥η(X)∥ ,


so η ∈ L2 (PN ; RN ) if and only if ΣN (X)1/2 η ∈ L2 (PN ; RN ). The map η 7→ ΣN (X)1/2 η is
therefore an eigenvalue-preserving bijection between the nonzero-eigenvalue eigenspaces of
the two operators. Finally, T̃ is self-adjoint on L2 (PN ; RN ) because k̃(X, X̃)′ = k̃(X̃, X). □


F    Finite-Cross-Section Menus

Throughout this section, N is fixed. The construction conditions are stated in Appen-
dices C.2.1, C.2.2, and C.2.3. Whenever an exact fixed-index limit exists, we write

              λj (ΨP )
         lim           = λ∗j (N ),   j fixed.                                              (F.1)
         P →∞     P

For partition menus the limit is along the specified sizes Pn . These constants agree with the
fixed-N definition in Theorem 1.




                                                 102
F.1   Settling menus and random features

This subsection proves the exact limit (F.1) for settling menus, whose empirical kernel (D.5)
converges as in (D.6). Paragraph (i) below derives that convergence for random labels, and
paragraph (ii) imposes it for deterministic ones. The managed-payoff space is (D.1).
   The constants λ∗j (N ) have a simple interpretation. Reweight the limiting menu into
combinations with zero pairwise second moments and order them by second moment: λ∗j (N )
is the second moment of the j-th such combination, the j-th principal-component portfolio
of the menu in the limit. Formally, λ∗j (N ) = λj (TC ) is the j-th eigenvalue of the integral
operator TC on L2 (P) with kernel C, the limit in (D.6). Equation (F.1) then says that the j-th
eigenvalue of ΨP is, for large P , about P λ∗j (N ). That infinitely many λ∗j (N ) are positive
is the statement that the menu keeps finding new return directions with positive second
moments however far down the list one looks.
   Kernel convergence makes the sequence λ∗1 (N ) ≥ λ∗2 (N ) ≥ · · · ≥ 0 square-summable,
so the constants decline to zero. The decay bounds their size rather than their number.
Infinitely many of them are strictly positive exactly when TC has infinite rank, which
completeness (C.4) on an infinite-dimensional D supplies (Lemma D.6).


(i) Random labels. Draw the labels θ1 , . . . , θP independently from µθ and set fk =
f (·; θk ). The trace condition (C.5), with Rθ = N −1 f (Xt ; θ)′ rt+1 , then delivers (D.6) through
a law of large numbers, with limit C = K. The positive-µθ -measure completeness condition
in Appendix C.2.1 verifies (C.4). Without (C.5), this route to Assumption 1 and the exact
limit is unavailable, although Assumption 1 need not fail. Under the weaker Hilbert–Schmidt
condition, Theorem E.5 still gives the lower bound.




                                                103
(ii) Deterministic labels. Kernel convergence (D.6) may instead be imposed directly.
Under ΣN (X) ⪯ Σ̄N IN , for a limit W ∈ L2 (p ⊗ p), let

                    1 X (P )     (P )
      WP (u, v) =         fk (u)fk (v),      C(Z, Z̃) = N −2 r′ [W(Xi , X̃i′ )]i,i′ r̃.
                    P k≤P


Conditioning as in (E.9) gives

                           Σ̄N
      ∥KP − C∥L2 (P⊗P) ≤       ∥WP − W∥L2 (p⊗p) .                                          (F.2)
                            N

If also ΣN (X) ⪰ σ 2 IN , the reverse bound holds with σ 2 /N .           Thus raw weight-kernel
convergence is sufficient under the upper bound and equivalent to kernel convergence under
both bounds. Conversely, applying the lower bound to KP − KP ′ makes WP Cauchy in
L2 (p ⊗ p). The upper bound then identifies the kernel limit with the displayed C.

here is ⟨·, TC ·⟩, so Lemma D.6 supplies this rank condition from completeness (C.4) on an
infinite-dimensional D. Under these hypotheses every λ∗j (N ) is strictly positive and every

eigenvalue of ΨP indexed by a fixed j grows at the rate P . Nothing here required the menu

Step 2: random labels. Let θ1 , . . . , θP be i.i.d. draws from µθ and fk = f (·; θk ), so that
KP of (D.5) is the Monte-Carlo kernel of the portfolio kernel K in (E.3). We claim that
condition (C.5) alone implies (D.6), with C = K. The convergence required is in L2 (P ⊗ P),
so the relevant law of large numbers is its vector-valued counterpart rather than the scalar
one: the strong law for i.i.d. random elements of a separable Banach space, due to Mourier
(1953). In its modern form (Ledoux and Talagrand, 1991, Corollary 7.10), if ξ, ξ1 , ξ2 , . . . are
i.i.d. random elements of a separable Banach space B, then n1 k≤n ξk → E ξ almost surely
                                                             P

in the norm of B if and only if ξ is Bochner integrable, that is, if and only if E∥ξ∥B < ∞
(Diestel and Uhl, 1977, Theorem II.2.2).
   We apply this with B = L2 (P ⊗ P), which is a separable Hilbert space, and ξθ := g(·; θ) ⊗
g(·; θ). Its norm is ∥ξθ ∥L2 (P⊗P) = ∥g(·; θ)∥2L2 (P) = E[g(Z; θ)2 ], so the Bochner-integrability
criterion reads

F.2       A fixed basis

                                (P )
The fixed-basis menu is fk (x) = P 1/2 qk Hk (x) of Appendix C.2.2, with {Hk } an orthonormal
basis of L2 (p) and {qk } bounded. Two things separate it from random features. The menu


                                                       107
of size P consists of the first P basis functions, not a fresh sample, so nothing is drawn at
random and there is no law of large numbers to appeal to. When inf k |qk | > 0, (D.6) fails,
so the route of Appendix F.1 is closed in that case. What replaces the law of large numbers
is monotonicity. Enlarging the menu adds one positive rank-one term, so every eigenvalue
can only rise, and the limit exists because the whole family is uniformly bounded.
   Use the pooled law p = N −1 i pi , where pi = law(Xi,t ); identical distributions across
                                   P

stocks are unnecessary. The conditional second moment obeys

condition on the stocks’ second moment alone: no factor structure is used anywhere in this
subsection. Write

For random features, the menu is an i.i.d. sample and the constants are the eigenvalues of a
compact limit reached by a law of large numbers. Here the menu is a fixed list, the constants
are reached from below by a monotone sequence, and the limiting operator need not be
compact, which is why the λ∗j (N ) of (F.11) carry no guarantee of decay beyond what {qk }
and M impose.


F.3     Partition menus

Sorted portfolios, the workhorse of empirical asset pricing, are the leading partition menu.
They are also the sharpest illustration of what this paper is about, because a sort refines:




                                                          112
the number of portfolios is the number of cells, so enlarging the menu means cutting the
characteristic space finer, and there is no separate dial to turn.

As a is arbitrary, every eigenvalue of P −1 ΨP is at least c− σ 2 /N > 0: the disjoint cells give

Why partitions need a separate argument. The bounds (C.10)–(C.11) and (C.8) put
each cell return’s second moment between positive multiples of P/N at fixed N . The floor
in Lemma F.3 implies


      ∥KP ∥2L2 (P⊗P) = tr((ΨP /P )2 ) ≥ P c− 2 σ 4 /N 2 −→ ∞.


Thus kernel convergence (D.6) fails, even though the operator can settle strongly. Every
eigenvalue diverges, a stronger conclusion than the fixed-rank statement for random features.
The fixed basis shares this floor when inf k |qk | > 0 (Proposition F.2). Partitions have no
separate coefficient sequence: refining their cells also increases the menu size.

The structure of the limiting spectrum. We now state what partitions yield beyond
(F.1). Fix N , and let p be the pooled characteristic law of Appendix C.2: the average
cross-sectional distribution of characteristics, and the distribution a sort is formed against
in practice. Nothing is assumed about how the stocks differ from one another; in particular,
they need not be exchangeable. The mass ceiling (C.8) makes the pooled characteristic law
nonatomic (Appendix C.2.3): no single value has positive probability under p. This permits
equal-mass cells along the dyadic subsequence. An atom of mass a > 0 would instead prevent
its containing cell from having mass 1/P < a. Nonatomic marginals alone do not exclude
identical realized characteristics across stocks; the pair-law condition imposed below controls
that possibility.
   Two distinct objects drive the spectrum, and both grow at rate P , so growth orders
cannot tell them apart. Averaging across the cross-section, let ς 2 on the one hand, and the




                                             116
pair law P2,N together with c on the other, be defined by

          Z

                 ς 2 dp =

             A              N i≤N
     Z

             c dP2,N =                     E 1A (Xi,t ) 1B (Xj,t ) [ΣN (Xt )]ij ,
       A×B                  N (N − 1) i̸=j


for Borel sets A, B, with P2,N := N (N1−1)
                                                P
                                                    i̸=j law(Xi,t , Xj,t ) the average law of the charac-

teristics of two distinct stocks at stock count N . The limiting pooled pair law remains P2 as
in (B.6). Thus ς 2 (x) is the conditional second moment of a single stock with characteristic
x, and c(x, x̃) is the conditional co-moment of two different stocks with characteristics x
and x̃; both are averaged over the cross-section, and each reduces to the obvious conditional
expectation when the stocks happen to be exchangeable. Let ϱ measure how far the charac-
teristics of two stocks are from being drawn independently of one another: ϱ ≡ 1 when they
are independent and identically distributed, and ϱ records their cross-sectional dependence
otherwise. Formally ϱ = dP2,N /d(p ⊗ p) is the density of P2,N against p ⊗ p, when that
density exists. For the whole-spectrum description, we additionally assume P2,N ≪ p ⊗ p
    RR
and    (cϱ)2 dp dp < ∞. These are additional comovement conditions, not consequences of
Appendix C.2.3. Its upper stock-moment bound does imply ess sup ς 2 < ∞. For N = 1,
omit the pair-law formulas and set the comovement kernel to zero; the own-variance formulas
remain valid.
   For both Hilbert spaces used here, set c̃ = (N − 1)cϱ/N . On L2 (p) or L2 (m), Tc̃ denotes
integration against that kernel using the measure of the stated space. The limiting cell-
uniform law is dm = dp/g; it is a probability law because dmP = dp/gP gives every current
cell mass 1/P and gP → g uniformly with a positive lower bound. Multiplication by a
function h is denoted Mh .

      P c− σ 2           P c+ Σ̄N

             PP

      These Courant–Fischer values satisfy λ∗j (N ) ≥ a+ and λ∗j (N ) ↓ a+ ; each value above
      a+ is a discrete eigenvalue, counted with multiplicity.

(iii) Kernel convergence (D.6) fails, although (C.2)–(C.3) hold.

   The law m is the limiting distribution of a uniformly selected current cell; it need not
assign equal probability to the cells of an earlier partition. The bulk in item (i) is the cell-
weighted own-second-moment distribution. Its support is bounded below by c− σ 2 /N . For
equal-mass cells it is the law of ς 2 (X)/N under p. Item (ii) describes the fixed-index top
of the spectrum. Its excess over a+ records comovement above the upper bulk edge; for
every δ > 0, only finitely many values exceed a+ + δ. There may be infinitely many positive
excesses converging to zero, or none. In the latter case the top of the spectrum is flat at



                                                    118
a+ , although the bulk need not be a point mass. Compact comovement can also produce
isolated eigenvalues below the lower bulk edge, which item (ii) does not describe.

The λ∗j (N ) therefore never descend into the bulk: they stall at its upper edge. Lemma A.5
also says which case obtains at each j: either λ∗j (N ) is a genuine eigenvalue strictly above
the bulk, or λ∗j (N ) has already reached the edge and λ∗j ′ (N ) = ess sup(gς 2 )/N for all j ′ ≥ j.
    Second, only finitely many exceed that edge by any fixed margin. If Cg = M√g Tc̃ M√g and
sj (Cg ) are its singular values, the min–max inequality gives λj (B) ≤ ess sup(gς 2 /N )+sj (Cg ).
Thus, for any δ > 0, the number of j with λ∗j (N ) > ess sup(gς 2 )/N + δ is at most #{j :
sj (Cg ) > δ}, which is finite by compactness. Those finitely many values are generated by the
reweighted comovement Cg , and they are item (ii) of the proposition. Negative comovement
can instead generate isolated values below the lower bulk edge; these are outside the fixed-
index top of the spectrum. The bulk itself, item (i), is a statement about the empirical
distribution of all P eigenvalues, whose indices grow with P , which is a different object from
the fixed-j limits λ∗j (N ).

Step 5: the bulk, item (i). Fix a truncation rank r and split EP = EP + Rr , where
  (r)
EP collects the r eigenvalues of EP largest in modulus. (Truncating the top r would not

                                                               
well.) Then ∥Rr ∥op = |λ|(r+1) (EP ) = P |λ|(r+1) (Tc̃ ) + o(1) , where |λ|(1) ≥ |λ|(2) ≥ · · ·
are the eigenvalues ordered by modulus, and these tend to zero by compactness. Two
standard facts about empirical spectral distributions are used, and we state them. The
rank inequality: for symmetric P × P matrices A, B, ∥F A − F B ∥∞ ≤ rank(A − B)/P (Bai
and Silverstein, 2010, Theorem A.43). And a Lévy-distance form of Weyl’s inequality: since
λj (A)−λj (B) is bounded by ∥A−B∥op for every j (Lemma A.7, part 1), the two distribution
functions are within ∥A − B∥op of one another in the Lévy metric. With these, the rank
inequality gives F ΨP /P − F (DP +Rr )/P ∞ ≤ r/P → 0, while Weyl’s inequality (Lemma A.7)
bounds the Lévy distance between the spectral distributions of (DP + Rr )/P and DP /P by
∥Rr ∥op /P = |λ|(r+1) (Tc̃ )+o(1). Letting P → ∞ and then r → ∞, and using |λ|(r+1) (Tc̃ ) → 0,
the spectral distributions of ΨP /P and of DP /P have the same weak limit. It remains to

Remark F.6 (Why a sort cannot settle to a compact kernel) The two summands
of Asort are of different type, and that is exactly why a refining sort falls outside the
settling framework (D.6) of Appendix F.1. The comovement Tc̃ is compact, its eigenvalues
decaying, the kind of operator a random-feature menu settles to. The own-variance block is
the opposite. It is the multiplication operator Mς 2 /N , whose essential spectrum ess ran(ς 2 /N )
is bounded away from zero, and it inherits this from the cell structure: the sort’s own-overlap
kernel

                        X 1{x ∈ Ck } 1{x̃ ∈ Ck }                      Z
      ksort (x, x̃) =                              ,   (ΠP ϕ)(x) =        ksort (x, x̃) ϕ(x̃) p(dx̃) ,
                         k
                                   p(Ck )


is the reproducing kernel of the projection ΠP (weight 1/p(Ck ) on each cell). Unlike a
                                                                       R
decaying compact kernel, a projection fixes the constant function, ksort (x, x̃) p(dx̃) =
P
   k 1{x ∈ Ck } = 1 for every x and every P , and as the cells refine ΠP → I, the identity,

whose spectrum is flat: every eigenvalue one, nothing decaying, no cliff. For (p ⊗ p)-almost
every pair of distinct points x ̸= x̃, the mass ceiling makes the nested cells eventually separate
them: p(C (P ) (x)) ≤ c+ /P → 0. Thus ksort (x, x̃) → 0, while the diagonal 1/p(Ck ) blows up
as the cells shrink, so the mass escapes to the diagonal, exactly where an L2 kernel cannot
follow it. Hence the empirical co-moment kernel of a refining sort does not converge to a
Hilbert–Schmidt C as (D.6) demands: its own-variance part converges instead to Mς 2 /N ,
which lifts every eigenvalue by the floor (F.16) rather than leaving a cliff. The row sums

                                               126
equal to one identify ΠP as a projection converging to the identity; they are not a near miss
of the settling condition.


Two stocks, in closed form. Proposition F.5 holds at any fixed N , and taking N = 2
makes every object in it explicit. Nothing in the construction depends on N being large: the
cells partition the characteristic space rather than the cross-section, so P → ∞ still means
cutting that space ever more finely, and the characteristics are still continuously distributed.
Most cells are empty in any given period, which is harmless, because ΨP is an expectation
over the characteristics rather than a realized matrix.
    The economy. One factor, unit betas, homoskedastic residuals: ri,t+1 = Gt+1 + εi,t+1 ,
where G is independent of the characteristics, E[G] = 0, E[G2 ] = 1, and the residuals
are independent of G and the characteristics, mutually uncorrelated, mean zero, and have
variance σ 2 > 0. Then

own variance ς 2 (x) = 1 + σ 2 , and the off-diagonal gives the comovement c(x, x̃) = 1.

   What produced them. The economy has two assets and one factor, and every stock
has the same beta. Yet the sort reports infinitely many diverging comovement directions,
                                                                               2
and (F.24) says exactly where they came from: the excess λ∗j (N ) − 1+σ

the strength of the j-th mode of cross-sectional dependence in the characteristics. Set θ = 0

2+σ 2                           2

                           for j ≥ 2: one comovement direction, the market, which is the
right answer for a one-factor economy. Every direction beyond the first is generated by
cross-sectional dependence in the characteristics.


The bulk can hide a factor entirely. Item (ii) of Proposition F.5 asserts only that
λ∗j (N ) ≥ a+ = ess sup(gς 2 /N ). Nothing need lie above it, and the failure is not pathological.
    One way is negative comovement. If Tc̃ ⪯ 0, so that stocks comove negatively in
every direction, then Cg ⪯ 0 and B ⪯ Mgς 2 /N in the notation of Step 4 of the proof of
Proposition F.5. Hence, for equal-mass cells,


        λ∗j (N ) = ess sup(ς 2 /N )   for every fixed j,


although the bulk may remain nonconstant. Negative comovement may also create a lower
outlier. For example, take N = 2, independent uniform characteristics, and

                           
                  2    −1
        Σ2 =               .
                  −1    2


For equal-mass cells, ΨP /P = IP − (2P )−1 11′ has P − 1 eigenvalues equal to one and one
eigenvalue equal to 1/2. Thus every fixed-index top limit is one, while a separated eigenvalue
persists below the bulk.
    The second way is more troubling, because the comovement is genuine and positive and
still does not show. In the example below, the factor loads only on low-variance stocks. Its
contribution is too small to lift that part of the spectrum above the higher variance elsewhere
in the cross-section. Take N = 2, characteristics independent and uniform, and


        β(x) = 0.3 · 1{x < 21 },      σε2 (x) = 0.10 · 1{x < 21 } + 3.00 · 1{x ≥ 12 },




                                                  129
so that Σ2 (X) is the one-factor matrix ββ ′ + diag(σε2 ). The factor is real: stocks with
characteristic below 21 load on it. Yet ess sup ς 2 /N = 1.5, while the whole comovement
contributes only 21 β 2 dp = 0.0225, concentrated exactly on the stocks whose own variance
                   R

is 0.19. The result is λ∗j (N ) = 1.5 for every j: nothing at all above the bulk. Move the
same factor onto the high-variance stocks instead and it reappears: the bulk edge rises
to (3.00 + 0.09)/2 = 1.545, and λ∗1 (N ) = 1.5675 sits above it. The factor’s comovement
contribution is unchanged; only its location has moved.
   The two failures point in opposite directions, and together they undermine any factor
count read off a sorted spectrum. A sort can manufacture diverging directions that are
not factors, as (F.24) shows; and it can conceal factors that are. Whether a true factor is
visible above the bulk turns on whether it happens to live among stocks whose idiosyncratic
variance is large enough to lift it clear, rather than on how strong it is.
   The lesson is that the cliff is a property of the menu rather than of the economy. A
menu confined to a fixed, finite set of weighting schemes spans only finitely many directions,
and so manufactures a finite factor model however rich the underlying cross-section. A
narrow positive feature bandwidth can instead produce an apparent finite-factor pattern, as
in Section 5.1, while retaining infinitely many positive limiting growth constants. A refining
sort goes to the other extreme and manufactures no cliff at all. Both are artifacts of the
weighting scheme. The refining sort also shows that (D.6) has content: the sort violates it,
and violating it removes the cliff rather than restoring it.
Without the generating condition (C.6), Step 3 of the proof of Proposition F.5 still gives
the fixed-index limits. A sort resolves only the comovement its own cuts can see: a sort
that refines size alone can separate almost every sampled pair when size has a nonatomic
distribution, but it does not recover functions of the other characteristics. By contrast, with
a positive continuous density on Rd , cycling through coordinates and bisecting each cell at
the conditional median produces a generating dyadic construction.



                                              130
   So the sort can produce a spectrum that looks exactly like an approximate factor struc-
ture: a handful of large eigenvalues, then a bulk.         That appearance is the trap.     An
approximate factor structure in the spectral sense of Chamberlain and Rothschild (1983)
requires the eigenvalues outside the handful to stay bounded, and here they do not: by (F.16)
every one of the P eigenvalues in the bulk diverges at the very same rate P as the handful
above it. The bulk is idiosyncratic risk levered up by the sort until it grows exactly as fast
as genuine comovement does, rather than idiosyncratic risk that washes out. A researcher
who finds a gap in this spectrum and concludes “k factors, and bounded idiosyncratic noise
below” has read the gap correctly and the noise catastrophically wrongly.
   The floor is a consequence of own-stock second moments and the menu normalization; it
does not by itself identify priced risk. Conditional on a cell holding one name, its return is a
P/N multiple of that stock’s return, so its conditional second moment is (P/N )2 [ΣN (X)]ii .
For an equal-mass cell, averaging the own-stock contribution over occupancy gives the
diagonal term (P/N )E[ς 2 | C]. Disjointness makes these own-stock contributions diagonal
across cells; full cell returns can still comove through different stocks.
   A refining sort violates the boundedness half of an approximate factor structure outright,
but for a mundane reason: each cell’s own second moment grows like P/N . The sort
spectrum illustrates the absence of a cliff without isolating the comovement mechanism
behind Theorem 1. Proposition F.5 does not say that the sort reveals the infinitely many
factors of a random-feature menu: the diverging bulk is the cross-section’s own idiosyncratic
variance, levered up, rather than new priced risk. Genuine comovement may be there
(item (ii) permits infinitely many directions to carry it), but the sort provides no way to tell
which eigenvalues carry it and which are levered noise, and, as the hidden-factor example
shows, it may reveal none of it at all. That is precisely why the eigenvalue cliff cannot be
used to certify a factor model, and why the granularity of a menu must be judged against
the size of the cross-section rather than trusted on its own.



                                              131
F.4    Standard-menu summary

Proposition F.7 (Standard menus satisfy Assumption 1 at fixed N ) At each fixed
N , the constructions in Appendices C.2.1, C.2.2, and C.2.3 satisfy Assumption 1 under
their stated conditions. For random features the conclusion holds almost surely for the infinite
independent label sequence; for partitions all limits are along the specified refining sizes Pn .
Each construction also has an exact fixed-index limit (F.1), and all these limits are positive.
The constant Cmenu may depend on N and, for random features, on the realized infinite label
sequence.

    The fixed-N conditions alone give neither uniformity in N nor the growing-stock bound
in Lemma D.8.

Proof of Proposition F.7. For random features, Lemma F.1 proves settling, complete-
ness, the operator bound, and exact convergence. For a fixed basis, these conclusions are
Proposition F.2. For partitions, Lemma F.4 gives Assumption 1, the exact limit, and its
positivity.                                                                                     □

   For the fixed basis, a sufficient bound uniform in N is supN (supk |qk |)2 Σ̄N /N < ∞. For
                                                    2
                                                                       R
random features, an integrable envelope supN E[Rθ,N    ] ≤ ē(θ) with ē dµθ < ∞ gives a
pathwise bound for all P, N by the scalar strong law. These are additional bounds on the
growing-stock sequence. For a random-feature menu the settled form is compact, its αj
decline to zero, and asymptotic completeness keeps them positive. For a refining sort the
settled form is not compact at all (Remark F.6), and the constants are held up from below
by leverage rather than by richness: αj ≥ c− σ 2 /N for every j. That floor is Lemma F.3.

Assumption G.1 (Managed-portfolio regularity) We add the following to the condi-
tional factor model of Assumption 3 and to clauses (ii)–(iv) of Assumption B.3, allowing
the common characteristic-law clause of Assumption 3 to be replaced by Assumption B.1.
Thus the appendix results invoking this assumption cover both the baseline common law
and fixed-effect-dependent characteristic laws. Clauses (i) and (v) of Assumption B.3 are

in Theorem 3 are separate from the managed-portfolio model space.

 (a) Idiosyncratic variance: there are constants 0 < σ 2 ≤ σ̄ 2 < ∞ with σ 2 ≤ σε2 (x, s) ≤
     σ̄ 2 for every fixed effect s and ps -almost every x. These are the residual bounds already
     imposed in Assumption 3 and recalled in Assumption B.3(iii).

 (b) Uniformly elliptic factor moments: let W denote the common shock of the cross-
     section (Assumption 3, or its extension B.1; see also Theorem B.2), PW its law, and
     require p(· | w) ≪ p for PW -almost every w, and set dw = dp(· | w)/dp. We require
     that the factor second moment be uniformly elliptic in the state (there are constants
     0 < ω ≤ ω̄ < ∞ with ω IK ⪯ Ω(w) ⪯ ω̄ IK for PW -almost every w) and that the
     conditional densities be uniformly square integrable over the state,


           ess sup ∥dw ∥L2 (p) < ∞ .                                                      (G.2)
             w

 (c) Nonatomic characteristics: there are a fixed effect s0 of positive limiting share
     πs0 > 0 and a vector a0 ∈ RK such that ps0 (a′0 B(·, s0 ) ̸= 0) > 0 and the restriction of

H.3    The large-cross-section limit for a partition menu

Proposition F.5 is a fixed-N statement, and its constants λ∗j (N ) depend on the cross-section:
they are the λ∗j (N ) = λj (Asort ) of (F.19), built from the two objects the sort cannot tell
apart: the levered own variance ς 2 /N and the dependence-adjusted comovement c̃ = NN−1 cϱ
between distinct stocks. This subsection lets N → ∞. The point is that the two objects
part company in the limit: the levered own variance is of order 1/N and washes out, and
what is left is exactly the comovement.

                                             147
   For a partition menu, Assumption G.2 asks that the own variance and the comovement
settle as the cross-section grows. The assumption represents all cross-sections on one fixed
pooled probability space (X, p) and holds the refining partitions (hence their limiting mass
density g) fixed as N varies. On that common space, it requires

                                ZZ
                2
                                                            2
     sup   sup ς(N )   < ∞,           c(N ) ϱ(N ) − c∞ ϱ∞        dp dp −−−→ 0 ,               (H.29)
      N                                                                N →∞


                                                                      RR
for a limiting dependence-adjusted comovement c∞ ϱ∞ with                   (c∞ ϱ∞ )2 dp dp < ∞, where
the subscript records the cross-section size. The common-p representation and the displayed
L2 convergence are triangular-array restrictions; they are not consequences of pointwise de
Finetti convergence alone. Under Assumption 3, the following conditions suffice for them.
The average marginal p, the pair density ϱ, and the partitions do not depend on N . With
several fixed effects, an N -free pair density restricts the fixed-effect composition, because
finite-N pair laws generally differ from their limit. Define the conditional factor moment
and its limit by


     c(N ) (z, u) = E[B(z, S1 )′ Ω(Xt )B(u, S2 ) | X1 = z, X2 = u],

       c∞ (z, u) = E[B(z, S1 )′ Ω(Wt )B(u, S2 ) | X1 = z, X2 = u].


The limit c∞ equals the posterior-mean form EW [β̄W (z)′ Ω(W )β̄W (u) | X1 = z, X2 = u].
Because B is bounded, (H.6) and dominated convergence give c(N ) → c∞ in the displayed
weighted L2 norm, which is the second half of (H.29). The first requirement is the uniform
variance bound of Assumption G.1(a) together with q̄ < ∞.

Two remarks. First, the levered noise that dominates the fixed-N spectrum (the floor (F.16)
and the bulk of Proposition F.5(i), both of order ς 2 /N ) disappears here. It is a fixed-N
artifact of the sort, and the large-N limit strips it away, leaving only comovement. The
artifact still matters at any finite N : comovement is visible only where it lifts an eigenvalue
above the levered-noise edge a+ , and Proposition F.5(ii) guarantees only λ∗j (N ) ≥ a+ .

                                              150
   Second, the limiting constants are the same object in both menu cases, up to the weighting
the menu applies: κj = λj (KH) for a random-feature menu (Proposition H.4) and κj =
λj (Mg H) for a partition menu, the refinement density g of (C.8) playing for the sort the role K
plays for a random-feature menu; for cells of equal mass g ≡ 1 and the sort’s weighting drops
out. Since K1/2 is injective and M√g is boundedly invertible, the two have infinitely many
positive constants under exactly the same condition, rank(H) = ∞. Genericity, however, is
proved below only for random-feature menus, in the regular configuration spaces of the next
subsection.


H.4       Genericity of the managed-portfolio factor count

This subsection shows that infinite rank of H, and hence infinitely many positive κj , is generic
in each regular configuration space. It defines these spaces, shows that each is a Baire space
of admissible models, and constructs infinite-rank configurations arbitrarily close to every
configuration.

Definition H.6 (Model configurations) Fix the portfolio weights f , the type shares

πℓ > 0, and the marginal laws pℓ , with p =  ℓ πℓ pℓ . An admissible model is a tuple

ϑ = (B, PW , {pℓ (· | w)}ℓ,w , Ω) satisfying Assumption 3, with its common characteristic-law
clause replaced by Assumption B.1, parts (ii)–(iv) of Assumption B.3, and Assumptions G.2,
H.2, and G.1. In particular, the type-specific conditional densities dℓ,w = dpℓ (· | w)/dpℓ
satisfy

      Z

The family is specified type by type; its pooled conditional law alone does not determine the
posterior loadings in (G.1). We do not impose Assumption B.3(i), which concerns the stock
factor count rather than the managed-payoff operator.

(H.13) and put

The spectral distance dsp is used only for approximation; it is not the topology defining gener-
icity. The eigenvalues of Aϑ are the constants κj in (H.16). By Lemma H.3, completeness
of the menu gives rank Aϑ = rank Hϑ .
   The regular configurations retain continuous positive conditional densities and uniform
moment bounds. They specify models directly; they are not identified through their co-
movement operators. Their primitive topology is stronger than convergence of the portfolio
spectra. The next lemma shows that every regular configuration has an admissible realization
and that MB is a Baire space.         The lemma after it shows that regular configurations
approximate all admissible spectra.

Lemma H.7 (A Baire space of admissible configurations) For every fixed admissible
B, the nonempty space MB is Baire. Every configuration has an admissible realization, and
the map ϑ 7→ Aϑ is continuous from dreg to the Hilbert–Schmidt norm.


Proof of Lemma H.7. Each Bochner L∞ space in E is Banach; separability of Cb is not
required for completeness. Indeed, a Cauchy sequence has a subsequence whose successive
essential-supremum distances are summable. Outside one null set this subsequence converges
uniformly in its Banach range, and its limit is strongly measurable and essentially bounded.
The full sequence then converges in the same norm. The two normalization maps in (H.31)
are continuous linear maps into L∞ ([0, 1]) and L2 (pℓ ), respectively. Their prescribed level
sets therefore form a closed affine subspace of E. The two strict uniform lower bounds
define an open subset of that subspace: a perturbation smaller than half either lower bound
preserves it. This subset is nonempty, since dℓ ≡ 1 and Ω
                                                        e ≡ IK belong to it. An open subset

of a complete metric space is a Baire space, which proves the first assertion.

                                              153
   To realize a configuration, draw G | U ∼ N (0, Ω(U
                                                  e )) independently of the characteristics

conditional on U , and use independent centered Gaussian residuals with the prescribed
bounded positive variances. The density constraints give exactly the fixed type marginals.
For each fixed N , the posterior factor second moment is

                  Z 1          Y
                        Ω(u)
                        e            dℓ(i) (u, Xi ) du
                   0           i≤N
      ΩN (X) =         Z 1Y                              .
                                dℓ(i) (u, Xi ) du
                        0 i≤N



Both numerator and denominator are continuous in the type empirical laws: for a.e. u, the
log densities are bounded continuous functions, and their uniform bounds permit dominated
convergence of the likelihood products. The denominator is bounded away from zero at each
fixed N . The within-type strong laws identify the vector of directing laws W from the infinite
characteristic sample. Conditional on W , that sample contains no additional information
about U . Because the stocks form one infinite array, the sigma-fields σ(X1 , . . . , XN ) increase.
Bounded martingale convergence yields ΩN (X) → E[Ω(U
                                                 e ) | W ] = Ω(W ) almost surely.

Conditioning preserves both ellipticity bounds; the factor means are zero. The uniform
density bounds imply (G.2).
                       R
   The fixed menu has ∥f (·; θ)∥2L2 (p) µθ (dθ) < ∞: this follows from the trace condition of

Lemma H.8 (Finite-state approximation) For every admissible model ϑ in Defini-
tion H.6 and every ϵ0 > 0, there is a finite-state regular configuration ϑ0 ∈ MB with
dsp (ϑ, ϑ0 ) < ϵ0 , with the same loadings B and fixed-effect marginals.

Proof of Lemma H.8. Write rℓ = dpℓ /dp and vℓ,w = πℓ rℓ dℓ,w , where dℓ,w = dpℓ (· | w)/dpℓ .
The nonnegative functions satisfy vℓ,w ≤ dw , so (G.2) puts them in L2 (PW ; L2 (p)) with
uniformly bounded statewise norms. Choose increasing finite sigma-fields Fn on the state
space such that conditional expectations of all vℓ,W and Ω(W ) converge in their respective

                                                        155
L2 spaces. The finitely many strongly measurable maps are essentially separably valued.
Their joint map therefore generates a countably generated sigma-field up to null sets; take
Fn generated by its first n generators.

Proposition H.10 (Generic infinite factor counts) Let MB be a regular configuration
                                                                        T
space. The sets Gj = {ϑ : κj (ϑ) > 0} are open and dense in MB , and G = j≥1 Gj = {ϑ :
rank Hϑ = ∞} is a dense comeager set. Its complement is not comeager, and G is not open.


Proof of Proposition H.10. Lemma H.7 and Weyl’s inequality (Lemma A.7, part 1) give
continuity of every κj in dreg , since


      |κj (ϑ) − κj (ϑ′ )| ≤ ∥Aϑ − Aϑ′ ∥op ≤ dsp (ϑ, ϑ′ ).


Thus each Gj is open. Completeness of the menu identifies its positive eigenvalue count with
rank Hϑ , so Lemma H.9 makes every Gj and their intersection dense. The complement of
G is a countable union of closed sets with empty interior, hence is meager. Since MB is a


                                                      159
nonempty Baire space by Lemma H.7, the whole space is not meager in itself. Consequently
the complement of G cannot also be comeager.
    Finally, truncate the witness in (H.34) after n signs, keeping Ω
                                                                   e = IK . The resulting

regular configuration has at most 2n directing states and therefore rank H ≤ K2n . Its
primitive distance to the witness is at most supk>n δk → 0, since the hk have disjoint supports.
An infinite-rank configuration is therefore approached by finite-rank configurations within
the same space, proving that G is not open.                                                   □


Remark H.11 (The growth constant is an iterated rather than joint limit) For
κj > 0, the reading λj (ΨP ) ∼ P κj of the limits in Propositions H.4 and H.5 is shorthand
for the two iterated steps: P → ∞ first, then N → ∞. It is not, by itself, a theorem
about a joint path (P, N ) → ∞, because that would require quantitative, uniform-in-N
control of the menu approximation. We neither assume nor prove that control in the general
model. Multiplicity also means that individual eigenvectors inside a limiting group need
not stabilize, although the group projection can.


I   Verification and Exact Double Limits

This appendix does two things. First, it verifies the quadratic-form convergence used in
Section 3 under explicit laws of large numbers: the conditional factor model of Assumption 3
delivers the convergence (D.7) of Assumption D.2. The factor structure alone supplies neither
the infinite-rank requirement (D.8) nor the menu exposure condition (D.9). Once these hold,
Lemma D.8 derives the uniform lower bound. Second, the appendix shows that the iterated
lower limits of Theorem 1 are exact limits for the three standard menus.

the argument above does not require that identification.                                     □

Proof of Theorem 5. We first identify the spectral groups, then show that every such
group can be priced by a compatible conditional mean. All second-moment hypotheses
remain unchanged when the means vary in P(Σ).

Step 5: truncation. Order the distinct positive eigenvalues of A by decreasing growth

                                                173
of the retained groups pairwise disjoint. The sum of their finite-menu squared uncentered

Sfull . Taking the iterated limits for these finitely many groups gives SJ ≤ Sfull . Then J → ∞
yields Ssys ≤ Sfull without interchanging an infinite sum with either cross-section limit.

there are none, both partial sums and xrsys are zero. Intersecting with {x̄r < 1} proves the
assertions in P r (Σ). For δ > 0, convergence holds throughout Pδr (Σ) and strictness holds on
its residual pricing set when there are infinitely many groups.
   The inequalities in (J.28) separate two possible shortfalls: the limiting groups need not
exhaust the large-stock inverse quadratic form, and the supremum over all stock counts need
not equal its lower limit. For the latter, take ΣN = 11′ + IN and µ̄N = aN 1, with a1 = 1
and aN = 1/5 for N ≥ 2. This second-moment family satisfies the stock hypotheses with a

J.3      Conditional factor-model calculations

The remaining calculations concern the conditional K-factor model. They describe its
limiting principal components and the smaller class of conditional means obtained by varying
factor premia. Genericity in this class needs its own reachability argument; it does not follow
by restricting a generic set of unrestricted stock means.

   For a growing stock count, P(Σ) requires the expected return of each fixed managed
weighting to converge, and Assumption D.2 controls its second moment. Under the condi-
tional factor-model conditions of Definition J.5, uniformly bounded individual-stock second
moments make the own-stock contribution of order 1/N for weightings with uniformly
bounded cross-sectional second moments. That contribution vanishes, while the co-moments
of distinct stocks can sustain systematic comovement and nonzero expected returns.

Proof of Lemma J.6. Put CP = FP H1/2 , so Ψ̄P = CP CP∗ and CP∗ CP = H1/2 FP∗ FP H1/2 .
The operator H1/2 is bounded and self-adjoint, so sandwiching the settled convergence (J.40)
between its two copies preserves the norm mode: P −1 CP∗ CP → H1/2 KH1/2 in operator
norm, with a compact limit (K compact, H1/2 bounded). By Lemma A.8 the limit’s nonzero
eigenvalues are those of A = K1/2 HK1/2 , the κj ; since the convergence is in operator norm
and the limit is compact, Weyl’s inequality gives λj (P −1 CP∗ CP ) → κj for each fixed j. Then
CP CP∗ shares the nonzero spectrum of CP∗ CP , so λj (Ψ̄P )/P → κj , which is (J.41). For the
group calculation put


   BP := P −1 CP∗ CP ,         B := H1/2 KH1/2 ,         aP := H1/2 (P −1 FP∗ FP )ϖ,   a := H1/2 Kϖ.
