   The set Pδ in Definition J.5 imposes a uniform second-moment margin. Every µG ∈ Pδ




                                                        190
satisfies


      Var(G | W ) = Ω(W ) − µG (W )µG (W )′ ⪰ δΩ(W ) ≻ 0       PW -almost surely.     (J.44)


Indeed, for a = Ω(W )−1/2 µG (W ), the smallest eigenvalue of I − aa′ is 1 − ∥a∥2 ≥ δ. This
proves (J.44) and excludes conditionally riskless nonzero-mean factor combinations. It does
not by itself exclude an ordinary nonnegative-payoff arbitrage; such a conclusion requires
information about the conditional distribution. For example, G = 2 with probability 1/4
and G = 0 otherwise has Ω = 1, µG = 1/2, positive variance, and a nonnegative nonzero
payoff. The Gaussian realization in the next proposition excludes such arbitrage. The
moment condition is sufficient for the Sharpe bounds proved below.

Proposition J.7 (A Gaussian realization) Fix the regular configuration ϑ of Defini-
tion J.5 and µG ∈ Pδ . Conditional on W , draw G independently of the characteristics
with law


      G | W ∼ N µG (W ), Ω(W ) − µG (W )µG (W )′ ,
                                                



and put εi = σε (Xi , Si )Zi , where the Zi are independent standard normals independent of
(W, X, G). Every nonzero finite or limiting managed return has positive probability of a
negative payoff. If, in addition, the type-specific conditional densities relative to pℓ are
continuous in characteristics and bounded uniformly over types and states between two
positive constants, the finite-N conditional factor moments are continuous empirical-law
functions, have the limits (H.6), and satisfy ∥ϖN − ϖ∥2 → 0.


Proof of Proposition J.7. Write V (w) = Ω(w) − µG (w)µG (w)′ ⪰ δΩ(w). A limiting
managed return is (ΓW ψ)′ G; conditional on W its variance is (ΓW ψ)′ V (W )ΓW ψ, which is
strictly positive wherever ΓW ψ ̸= 0. A nonzero limiting return has such a set of positive


                                            191
probability, and a nondegenerate normal variable has positive probability of a negative value.
At finite N , conditional on (X, W ), the independent residuals contribute variance

  Under the stated density bounds, write dℓ,w = dpℓ (· | w)/dpℓ . The posterior factor
                                      Q
moments are ratios with denominator EW i≤N dℓ(i),W (Xi ) > 0 and numerators obtained by
inserting µG (W ) or Ω(W ). These are continuous functions of the empirical laws at fixed N :
the log densities are bounded continuous functions and dominated convergence applies to
their likelihood products. The typewise strong laws identify the canonical directing-law state
from the infinite characteristic sample. The sigma-fields σ(X1 , . . . , XN ) increase because the
samples are initial segments of one infinite array. Bounded martingale convergence with this
filtration therefore gives (H.6).

Proposition J.9 (Generic factor premia) Let Nmax = ∞, fix δ ∈ (0, 1), and impose

Proof of Proposition J.9. Write µP,N and ΨP,N for the moments of Section 5.2 at stock

the uncentered ratios (u′j µP,N )2 /λj (ΨP,N ). Lemma J.8 supplies the centered maximal Sharpe

   For the group ratios we take P → ∞ at fixed N , followed by N → ∞. The Sharpe

   Fix a distinct positive eigenvalue κE of A. Choose a closed interval IE ⊂ (0, ∞) whose

     ∥PE,N − PE ∥op −→ 0.                                                            (J.49)


This formulation permits the eigenvalue κE to have any finite multiplicity and permits the
corresponding finite-N eigenvalues to split.
   Choose NE so that the endpoints of IE lie in the resolvent of AN and rank PE,N = rank PE
for every N ≥ NE . At each fixed N ≥ NE , the trace condition and the weight-kernel law
of large numbers make the matrix–operator dictionary applicable with H replaced by QN
and ϖ by ϖN . For a random-feature menu, this holds almost surely over the label draws.

   The scalar function equal to x−1 on IE and zero on a neighborhood of σ(A) \ IE is
continuous on a neighborhood of the spectra in (J.48). Continuous functional calculus,

   A generic premium prices every group. Each ℓE is a bounded linear map on the

   Fix δ ∈ (0, 1) and use the admissible premium space Pδ in (J.32). Lemma J.8 already
supplies its quantitative Sharpe bound; it remains to establish generic pricing inside this
space. When PW is nonatomic, Pδ has empty interior in L2 (PW ; RK ) (an L2 -small spike
on a set of small PW -mass leaves L2 -distance small but violates the pointwise bound), so a
property comeager in the ambient L2 can miss Pδ entirely; the argument must therefore run
inside Pδ . Now Pδ is convex (the map µG 7→ ∥µG (W )∥Ω(W )−1 is a seminorm pointwise, and
the constraint is its sublevel set) and closed in L2 (PW ; RK ): every convergent sequence has
an almost-surely convergent subsequence, and the pointwise constraint passes to its limit.
Consequently, a closed subset of a Hilbert space is complete, so Pδ is a complete metric space
and hence a Baire space. Within Pδ , fix a group E. The set NE := {µG ∈ Pδ : ℓE (µG ) =
0} = ker ℓE ∩ Pδ is closed and convex, and it is nowhere dense in Pδ . The probe is uniformly
bounded in its conditional second-moment norm. Indeed, with Cd = ess supw ∥dw ∥2 ,

                      √
     ∥ΓΩ
       wK
          1/2
              ζ∥ ≤     ω̄ LB Cd ∥K1/2 ζ∥2 =: Cζ < ∞.

                                                              √
Since κE > 0, Cζ > 0. For µG,0 = Ω1/2 ΓΩWK
                                            1/2
                                                ζ, set µ̂G,0 = 1 − δ µG,0 /Cζ . Then µ̂G,0 ∈ Pδ
                 √
and ℓE (µ̂G,0 ) = 1 − δ κE ζ/Cζ ̸= 0. All functions in this construction are measurable with
respect to the canonical directing-law state.

                                                198
    To see that NE has empty interior in Pδ , take any µG ∈ NE (so ℓE (µG ) = 0) and
any ε > 0. The convex combination µG,η = (1 − η)µG + η µ̂G,0 , η ∈ (0, 1), lies in Pδ
(convexity) and satisfies ℓE (µG,η ) = (1 − η)ℓE (µG ) + η ℓE (µ̂G,0 ) = η ℓE (µ̂G,0 ) ̸= 0 for η > 0,
with ∥µG,η − µG ∥ → 0 as η → 0, so every Pδ -ball around µG meets the complement of
NE . Thus NE contains no Pδ -ball; being also closed, it is nowhere dense in Pδ . The groups
                 S
are countable, so E NE is meager in Pδ ; off it, for a premium generic in Pδ , PE β ̸= 0
simultaneously for every group, i.e. SR2E,∞ > 0 for all E. Genericity is thus stated in Pδ , a
closed convex subset of a Hilbert space, hence complete and a Baire space, whose elements
satisfy the uniform second-moment margin. The sets Pδ increase as δ ↓ 0, and their union
                            ′ −1
S
  δ>0 Pδ = {µG : ess supW µG Ω µG < 1} sits inside the premia with positive conditional

factor covariance {µ′G Ω−1 µG < 1} without exhausting them: a premium whose pointwise
bound approaches one without attaining it belongs to the latter and to no Pδ . The statement
is made at a fixed δ because the proof uses the completeness of that particular closed set;
we make no category claim for the larger union. Finally, for a model generic in the sense
of Proposition H.10, infinitely many κj are positive, and a compact operator has finite
multiplicities at its nonzero eigenvalues, so there are infinitely many distinct positive κE ,
infinitely many groups, every one priced. Hence infinitely many principal-component groups
have nonzero premium projections, so no finite collection of those groups spans the limiting
premium profile.
    The category conclusion concerns the limiting premium space. For each such premium
and each realization satisfying Definition J.5, (J.51) transfers the group projections to the
iterated finite-menu limits.                                                                       □

     Both kernels are Gram kernels. Their diagonal integrals are bounded because |f | ≤ 2,
       √
|f¯| ≤ 2, 0 < v ≤ 1, and σε2 (X) ≤ σ̄ 2 . For either label-space Gram operator, Parseval and
                     R
Tonelli give tr T = k(θ, θ) µθ (dθ), proving the stated bounds. Subtracting Tk̃W from LN
and bounding the norm of each positive operator by its trace gives the convergence claim.
□

                                                     203
Remark K.4 (The order of limits) The limits in Theorem 4 and in the propositions
below are iterated, in the order of (11) with Nmax = ∞: P → ∞ first at a fixed cross-
section, then N → ∞. At each fixed N , the inner menu limit is governed, by (K.3), by
the operator LN of Lemma K.3, and that lemma gives LN → Tk̃W in operator norm. The
menu mean vectors computed below do not depend on N . For an isolated group of positive
eigenvalues, the Riesz projections of LN therefore converge in operator norm to those of Tk̃W ;
because the group is bounded away from zero, the inverses restricted to the group converge as
well. Applying the empirical Gram-operator limit first at fixed N and then letting N → ∞
thus justifies computing each group-pricing functional from the limiting systematic operator.
At finite N , the inner limit still contains the own-name contribution in (K.3); the geometric
staircase is the limiting systematic spectrum.

Step 5: odd rungs, by coordinatewise symmetry. Substituting (K.6) into Step 3 and
squaring gives, for even m, the closed form in (K.5), the sum running over the n ∈ Zd≥0 with
|n| = m/2. For odd m the rung earns nothing. The clean way to see this is a symmetry
that does not depend on the basis. The law N (0, Id ) of W and the kernel k, which depends
on W, W ′ only through ∥W − W ′ ∥2 , are both invariant under the reflection Wi 7→ −Wi of
any single coordinate i. So Tk commutes with each of the d reflections, each eigenspace Em
decomposes into their joint parity classes, and the constant function 1 lies in the all-even


                                                213
class. Hence PEm 1 picks up only the part of Em that is even in every coordinate separately.
In the Hermite basis this is exactly the vanishing of ⟨eW
                                                        k , 1⟩ whenever some ki is odd, a

stronger statement than vanishing when |k| is odd, and the reason the reflection must be
applied coordinatewise rather than globally as W 7→ −W . When m is odd, every k with
|k| = m has some ki odd, so PEm 1 = 0 and SR(m) = 0.

Step 6: the total. Every νm = ν0 Qm is strictly positive, and the Hermite eigenfunctions
form a complete orthonormal basis of L2 (N (0, Id )), so the eigenspaces Em exhaust the space
and every eigenfunction corresponds to a limiting principal component. Parseval applied to
the constant function is basis free, m≥0 ∥PEm 1∥2 = ∥1∥2L2 = 1, so summing (K.5) over m

   The parity structure is where the general case parts company with Proposition K.7.
When η = 0, the generating function is even in t and its odd coefficients vanish, so the
odd-degree components are unpriced. By (K.12), η = 0 exactly when the two offsets satisfy
κς = (2aH +κ)τ , in particular when both vanish, s = 0 and ς = 0. For κ = 0, the condition is
as = 0, and ς is immaterial. With s = 0 and constant h, (K.11) reduces to (K.13), which has
the shape of (K.6) with Q replaced by the premium rate Q̃feat,a . The premium rate exceeds
Qfeat,a when a > 0. Away from the offset-tuning condition, the eigenfunctions (K.10) need
no longer be even or odd about the origin and the linear term ℓH ηt/bI survives. For h0 ̸= 0,
every degree then earns a premium except on the additional Hermite-zero parameter sets on
                            √
which c < 0 places ℓH η/(2bI −c) at a zero of Hk . Thus s ̸= 0 is sufficient only away from
those offset-tuning and Hermite-zero sets. The eigenvalues, by Step 3, are indifferent to all
of this.                                                                                                         □

Proof of Proposition K.9. Step 1: the model satisfies the hypotheses. Here K = 1
and B ≡ 1. Lemma K.1 gives the finite-panel moment ΩN (X) = E[v(W ) | X] ∈ (0, 1]
and its limit v(W ). Hence the conditional second moment of the returns is ΣN (X) =
ΩN (X) 11′ + diag(σε2 (Xi )) ⪰ σ 2 IN , which is the ellipticity floor (C.10), and a stock’s own
                              2                               2             2
conditional second moment is ς(N ) (z) = E[v(W ) | Xi = z] + σε (z) ≤ 1 + σ̄ , hence is bounded

uniformly in N . The characteristics are continuously distributed, so nested cells of equal


                                               223
mass 1/P exist along the dyadic subsequence P = 2n . In particular, the coordinate-cycling
construction in Section 5.1 becomes the usual dyadic rectangular partition of (0, 1)d after
applying the standard normal distribution function to each coordinate. Every coordinate is
split infinitely often, so the rectangle diameters tend to zero; their generated sigma-field is the
Borel sigma-field, verifying (C.6). Equal cell masses give (C.8) and (C.9) with gP = g = 1.
The comovement between two distinct stocks is c∞ (x, x̃) = E[v(W ) | X1 = x, X2 = x̃],
bounded by one, and the average marginal p = N (0, Id ) and the pair law P2 are free of N
too, so the second half of (H.29) holds trivially and the first half is the uniform bound just
                        RR
recorded. Finiteness of (cϱ)2 dp dp is verified in Step 2.

Step 2: the dependence between two stocks. By (19), X1 and X2 are jointly Gaussian,
each marginally N (0, Id ), with E[X1,i X2,l ] = ρ δil : the coordinates are independent across i,
and within a coordinate the pair (X1,i , X2,i ) is bivariate standard normal with correlation ρ.
Mehler’s formula (Szegő, 1975, Problems and Exercises, Problem 23) expands the density of
a bivariate standard normal against the product of its marginals as

                                                                                      √

the formula reduces to Q ∼ ργ 2 at a = 0 (Proposition K.6). Thus each such drop sharpens
without bound: the leading modes dominate and the remainder collapses, i.e., an arbitrarily
sharp cliff.

Step 6: the shallow staircase, in log–log chord slopes. Plot the spectrum as the points
(j, κj ); its corners sit at the right ends of the rungs, (Jm , ν0 Qm ). In log–log coordinates a

      (log ν0 + (m+1) log Q) − (log ν0 + m log Q)       log Q

The denominator log(1 + m+1 ) strictly decreases in m, hence ζm strictly increases in m:

bounded away from 0, up to Qfeat,∗ (ρ) < 1) holds each ζm finite, a shallow staircase, while
γ → 0 sends Qfeat,a → 0 and every ζm → ∞, recovering the cliff. (For instance Qfeat,∗ (0.5) =
        √
0.5/(1 + 0.75) ≈ 0.27.)

the same staircase and differ only in its ratio. The comparison (24) is Proposition K.9(i′ ).
Both menus use the same iterated order, first P → ∞ at fixed N and then N → ∞, as
established above. The partition constants at finite N obey λ∗j (N ) ≥ σ 2 /N by (F.16) for
these equal-mass cells, and (K.21) describes their limits as N → ∞.                            □

Centered features. In the scalar model B ≡ 1 with a = 0, centering the features replaces
their state-side operator by ΠTk Π, where Π = I −1⊗1, because EW [f¯(θ; W )] = EX [f (X; θ)].
This compression has the same nonzero spectrum as the rank-one downdate


         1/2   1/2            1/2      1/2
        Tk ΠTk       = Tk − (Tk 1) ⊗ (Tk 1).


The difference ΠTk Π − Tk itself need not have rank one. Since 1 is not an eigenfunction of
Tk , centering does not simply delete an eigenfunction. The next lemma gives the retained
Hermite values and the interlacing secular roots; the ordered spectrum need not have adjacent
ratio Q. For nonconstant loadings or a > 0, feature centering does not give this scalar
compression.

Lemma K.11 (The centered spectrum) Let Tk =               m≥0 ν0 Q PEm with Q ∈ (0, 1) and

dim Em = gd (m), let 1 be a unit vector with wm := ∥PEm 1∥2 positive for even m and zero for
odd m, and let Π = I −1⊗1. Then the spectrum of ΠTk Π on 1⊥ consists of the accumulation
point 0 and the multiset union of the following nonzero values:


                                               231
  (i) gd (m) copies of ν0 Qm for odd m, and gd (m) − 1 copies for even m; and

 (ii) exactly one further eigenvalue in each interval (ν0 Q2j+2 , ν0 Q2j ), j ≥ 0, the roots of the
      secular equation i≥0 w2i (ν0 Q2i − λ)−1 = 0.
                       P


Multiplicities add when a root in (ii) equals an odd-degree value in (i); 0 is not an eigenvalue
on 1⊥ .

Proof of Lemma K.11. On 1⊥ the operator ΠTk Π is the compression of Tk , so we describe
that. For odd m, PEm 1 = 0, hence Em ⊆ 1⊥ , and Tk acts there as ν0 Qm , contributing gd (m)
copies. For even m, put um := PEm 1 ̸= 0; then Em ⊖ span(um ) ⊆ 1⊥ is Tk -invariant with
eigenvalue ν0 Qm , contributing gd (m)−1. This is (i), and it exhausts 1⊥ except for the closed
span U of {u2j }j≥0 intersected with 1⊥ , which has codimension one in U because 1 ∈ U.
                                                                          √
   On U the operator Tk is diagonal in the orthonormal basis û2j = u2j / w2j , with eigenval-
                                  √
ues ν0 Q2j , and 1 has coordinates w2j . If x ∈ U ∩1⊥ satisfies ΠTk x = λx, then Tk x = λx+µ1
                                               √                −1
with µ = ⟨1, Tk x⟩, so coordinatewise xj = µ w2j ν0 Q2j − λ . Imposing ⟨1, x⟩ = 0 gives
µ j w2j (ν0 Q2j −λ)−1 = 0, and µ = 0 would force x = 0; hence λ solves the secular equation.
  P

Its left side is strictly increasing in λ on each interval between consecutive ν0 Q2j , running
from −∞ to +∞ there, so it has exactly one root in each, which is (ii).
   Two consequences follow, and one does not. The top level disappears: gd (0) = 1, so (i)
contributes no copy of ν0 . The retained Hermite values remain ν0 Qm at their original degree
indices, and the secular roots lie between consecutive even levels. If a root equals an odd
level, its copy is added to that level’s multiplicity. Adjacent eigenvalues of the fully ordered
spectrum generally do not have ratio Q. Positivity of Tk gives ⟨x, ΠTk Πx⟩ = ⟨x, Tk x⟩ > 0
for every nonzero x ∈ 1⊥ , so zero is only a spectral accumulation point. Codimension-one
interlacing, λj+1 (Tk ) ≤ λj (ΠTk Π) ≤ λj (Tk ), is a coarser consequence of (i)–(ii); it also follows
directly from the variational formula in Lemma A.5.
   One thing that (i)–(ii) do not by themselves give is that the degree-one level tops the
centered spectrum. The single root µ1 of (ii) in (ν0 Q2 , ν0 ) may sit above ν0 Q, and where it

                                                 232
sits depends on the weights: since the left side of the secular equation increases through the
gap, µ1 < ν0 Q holds exactly when

      X     w2i                                   1−QX      w2i
           2i
                > 0            ⇐⇒         w0 >                     ,                         (K.28)
      i≥0
          Q −Q                                     Q i≥1 1 − Q2i−1

the two forms being related by Q2i − Q = −Q 1 − Q2i−1 and division by 1 − Q > 0. The
criterion can fail if w0 is small enough relative to w2 : at Q = 21 with (w0 , w2 ) = ( 21 , 12 ) and
no higher weights, the sum on the left of (K.28) is 1 − 2 = −1. Adding sufficiently small
positive weights at every higher even level and renormalizing preserves this strict sign. For
the Hermite weights (K.6), w2n = (1 − Q2 )d/2 Q2n (d/2)n /n!, where (z)n is the rising factorial.
At small Q and fixed d, the first two contributions to the left side of (K.28) are approximately
1 and −Qd/2. At the rounded parameters of Figure 1, d = 50, the criterion is about 0.76 for
Q = 0.010 and −3.08 for Q = 0.186. Thus the degree-one level tops the centered spectrum
at the narrow bandwidth, whereas the largest secular root tops it at the wide bandwidth. □


K.1    Premia, Sharpe ratios, and spanning

Two pieces of the premium calculation appear earlier, because they use the eigenbases con-
structed there: Proposition K.7 computes the rung premia in the constant-second-moment
case, and Step 6 of Proposition K.8 carries them to a state-dependent factor. What follows
collects the consequences, which Section 5.2 uses.
   Theorem 4 reports only eigenvalues. The same model also delivers the rewards attached
to those eigenvalues in closed form, and with them the two statements the paper needs: that
no finite set of principal components spans expected returns exactly, and that the limiting
maximal Sharpe ratio remains finite under the strict moment condition. We collect them
here, in the random-feature case (21) and in the order of limits of Theorem 4(a).
   Because the degree-m level is shared by many components, the individual components
within a rung are not separately identified, and the premia are stated for the rung as a

                                                233
whole.23 Write SR(m) for the rung-level uncentered ratio of degree m, SJ for the maximal
centered Sharpe ratio from the first J limiting principal components, restricted to rung
boundaries J = Jm , and Ssys := supJ SJ for the supremum over their finite linear combina-
tions.

L    Finite Samples Spread the Spectrum

The spectrum of Section 5.1 is a population object; in practice one sees the sample second-
moment matrix Ψ̂P = T1 Tt=1 Rt Rt′ from a panel of length T , and a natural worry is that
                        P

estimation noise might carve a cliff back into a shallow population staircase. Two rigorous
but distinct benchmarks are useful. First, in expectation, convex spectral functionals of the
sample matrix exceed their population counterparts, with the top and bottom eigenvalues
moving outward in expectation. Second, in a centered independent-component model with
P/T → y < 1, the whitened sample covariance is bounded above and away from zero with
high probability; Loewner order then prevents a bounded population eigenvalue ratio from
diverging in sample. Neither statement rules out a striking gap in one particular realization,
and the second does not cover the paper’s nonzero-premium, dependent managed returns.
    We adopt the standard model of the sampling noise: the returns are drawn as Rt =
        1/2
µP + VP zt , where µP = E[Rt ] and VP = Var(Rt ) are the managed-return mean and
covariance. The vector zt has mean zero and identity covariance, so E[Rt Rt′ ] = ΨP . Part (b)
sets µP = 0 and additionally requires i.i.d. entries from a fixed distribution with finite fourth
moment. Managed portfolios do not literally satisfy this model. Their entries are smooth
nonlinear functions of a common state and a common factor, hence strongly dependent. We
treat the model as a stylized benchmark rather than a description.

Theorem L.1 (Spectral dispersion and a centered no-split benchmark) Consider
a panel of independent and identically distributed returns R1 , . . . , RT with

                                      T
                                      X
      E[Rt Rt′ ] = ΨP ,    Ψ̂P = T1         Rt Rt′ ,
                                      t=1


and write ΨP = VP + µP µ′P with µP = E[Rt ] and VP = Var(Rt ).

                                                       236
 (a) Dispersion. For every convex φ, E tr φ(Ψ̂P ) ≥ tr φ(ΨP ), with strict inequality for
      φ(x) = x2 unless Ψ̂P = ΨP almost surely. Also, E tr Ψ̂P = tr ΨP , E λ1 (Ψ̂P ) ≥ λ1 (ΨP )
      and E λP (Ψ̂P ) ≤ λP (ΨP ). For Gaussian returns Rt ∼ N (µP , VP ) the second spectral
      moment is inflated by exactly

                                   1h                                ′
                                                                            i
            E tr Ψ̂2P − tr Ψ2P =             2      2         2
                                     (tr ΨP ) − tr ΨP + 2 tr VP + 4 µP VP µP .                  (L.1)
                                   T


 (b) No manufactured cliff in the centered independent-component benchmark.
                                      1/2
      Suppose µP = 0 and Rt = VP zt , where the entries of the P ×T matrix Z = [z1 , . . . , zT ]
      are i.i.d. from a fixed distribution with mean zero, variance one, and finite fourth
      moment. Let P, T → ∞ with P/T → y ∈ (0, 1). If j = jP < P satisfies λj+1 (VP ) > 0
      and λj (VP )/λj+1 (VP ) ≤ C0 for all sufficiently large P , then λj (Ψ̂P )/λj+1 (Ψ̂P ) = OP (1).
                                               √                     √
      More precisely, for every 0 < c < (1− y)2 and C > (1+ y)2 , with probability tending
      to one, simultaneously at every such rank,

             λj (Ψ̂P )        C λj (VP )    C
                          ≤                ≤ C0 .                                               (L.2)

   Part (b) is deliberately narrower than part (a). Its point is conditional: when the
whitened sample covariance is uniformly bounded above and away from zero, congruence
by an arbitrary population covariance cannot turn a bounded population eigenvalue ratio
into a divergent sample ratio. The standard extreme-eigenvalue law for an i.i.d. matrix
with finite fourth moment supplies exactly those two bounds when y < 1 (Yin, Bai and
Krishnaiah, 1988; Bai and Yin, 1993; Bai and Silverstein, 2010). No spiked-population
hypothesis, limiting bulk law, or outlier classification is needed.
   Neither part proves that a particular realized sample cannot display a conspicuous local
gap. Part (a) is an expectation/convex-order statement and part (b) applies only to the
centered independent-component benchmark. Managed returns have a nonzero mean and

                                                237
dependent whitened coordinates, so extrapolating (L.2) to them would require additional
assumptions.

Ψ̂P = ΨP almost surely. For φ(x) = x2 alone the strictness is the identity E tr Ψ̂2P − tr Ψ2P =
E∥Ψ̂P − ΨP ∥2F , which is positive unless Ψ̂P = ΨP almost surely. Taking φ(x) = ±x gives
the mean identity. The same Jensen step applied to A 7→ λ1 (A), which is convex, and to
A 7→ λP (A), which is concave, gives E λ1 (Ψ̂P ) ≥ λ1 (ΨP ) and E λP (Ψ̂P ) ≤ λP (ΨP ): the
extremes of the spectrum move outward.

Remark L.2 (Why P > T is different) The restriction y < 1 in part (b) is essential. If
P > T , then Ψ̂P is a sum of T rank-one matrices and therefore has rank at most T : it has
at least P − T zero eigenvalues. If the T observed return vectors are linearly independent,
as they are almost surely under an absolutely continuous joint law, its rank is exactly T
and the eigenvalue ratio at rank T is infinite. This hard cliff is created by the sample size
alone. It is a reminder that in the high-dimensional regime (the one of Section 5.4), the
sample spectrum beyond rank T carries no information about the population spectrum, and
Theorem 4 is a statement about the population.

   A short panel adds substantial spectral noise. The missing cliff of Theorem 4 remains a
population statement: part (a) says that spectral dispersion is inflated in expectation, while
part (b) says that a bounded population ratio cannot diverge in the centered independent-
component benchmark with y < 1. When P > T , the sample cliff at rank T of Remark L.2
is a fact about the panel rather than the economy. In both regimes, the covariance problem
of Section 5.4 has a sample part and a population part, and the population part does not go
away as T grows.




                                             240
